"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Mrs. Main, the editor of that bright little mountain paper the" Alpine Post" has probably more" first win- ter ascents" standing to her credit than any other woman living, and each winter that passes adds to the number ot them. Mrs. Main resides occasionally at Brighton, but the best part of her time is spent at Saint Moi eta in the Engadir e. Among French women moun- t tineers, the most illustrous is perhaps Mademoiselle Mary Paillon. She belongs to the French Alpine Club, svhicth unlike ours, admits women to memtership, and encourages them to accompany their male relatives to the mountain top. A great authority on " Scientific Mountaineering " of opinion that the modern race of young women shows as much intrepidity, coolness,and skill,as men do in the most ordinary climbing. Furthermore, he says it is very seldom that a woman when she has made up her mind to reach some peak, turns back, as a good many of the opposite sex do ; and above all, women grumble at the obstacles far less than do men. As regards caution, he goes on to remark that women have a long way the ad- vantage of men, and of the large numbers of British and American women, who have surmounted the most dan- gerous Alpine peaks, but few have come to grief.as com- pared with the opposite sex. Among the young married beauties of the past London season, the Duchess of Portland, Lady Helen Vincent, and Lady Naylor-Leyland stood out conspicuously ; ana of the tins, far away the most beautiful was Lady Alice Montagu. Great sympathy has been felt for the Duchess of Manchester during her daughter's illness, but the lovely young debutante is fast recovering, and so soon as she is able to travel, will be taken abroad to recruit her strength. Lady Alice has the most delicate but brilliant colouring, cheeks tinted like a rose, and scarlet lips, with quantities of golden brown hair, and lovely eyeti. The Duchess of Newcastle, who with her father Major Camay, is now at Homburg, is far from well. At the commencement of the year she was somewhat seriously indisposed, and has never really regained her strength. It does not appear to be generally known that Mrs. Candy, mother of the young Duchess, is an exceedingly good shot with a title, in fact she could hold her own with many a member of the sterner sex in this some- what rare accomplishment. She is a good all-round sportswoman, and like all the Rossmore extremely fond of horses and hunting, a taste which her Glace of Newcastle has inherited. _

Sail-brown linen is, at the present moment, enjoying considerabte favour for seaside and country gowns. This is both uncommon, and effective in appearance, and I strikes a welcome net° of change and variety. For yachting, pure and simple, the ever useful navy-blue serges continue to reign supreme although many frocks ia white and cream serge are being worn, and here and there a bright scarlet toilette makes its appearance. The newest serge and cloth gowns hay.) perfectly plain skirts, innocent of any sort of trimming beyond perhaps lines of stitching nar the hem. A iew only have made their appearance with flounces. These are decidedly ugly and heavy locking, besides being altogether inappro- priate. Numbers of women may still be seen in the streets ex- hibiting dusty irillings, and discolored hems, making one long for a sumptuary law,aod summary action with a pair of sharp shears, but many very up-to-date skirts for autumn wear, are, happily, being cut to oleic toe ground, and it would seem as though we were at last tiring of a mode that turns us into road and pavement sweepers. It is almost as much trouble, it is true, to persuade a builder of gowns to arrange one's skirt to escape the ground as it is to get a rocket inserted, but , if we study our health we should be firm upon the point, and insist upon our wishes being carried out. ! There is Ins doubt that artificial albumen in tabloid ! form might prove a boon and a blessing when on a journey, up a mountain or in a desert. One could stow away so many meals,and not feel the least inconvenience One's purse, or a tiny pocket would be all the accom- modation necessary for many days' rations. In times of war too it would surely be a capital thing for armies. But in the general way,it is improbable that the majority would rush for it. To sit down to a tabloid for break- i fast, a tabloid for lunch, and a tabloid for dinnsr,would scarcely seem a fascinating proceeding, The fashion of the day in serving dinner a la Reese, favours appreciably the decoration of the table with flowers,which suffer exceedingly tram proximity to hot foods. Only the (lessen appears upon the table now,and this is arranged in the most delicate of dishes,with little open saucers for sweetmeats, devilled almonds, and other gastronomic novelties,which were unknown to our forefathers, to whcm also the profusion of flowers appearing in every oay life nowadays, would have seemed astonishing and almost sinful wastefulness. But we have increased our expenditure in very nearly every department of life.and in nothing is this more appar- ent than in the service and appointments of the dinner table.

here is the recipe for a delicious sweet. Cut six sponge cakes into sr c ,s,lnd in an earthenware dish put alter- nate layers of cake,and bananam,slicest. Make a custard of the yolks of two eggs,sugar t) taste, and one pint of milk,flavoured with the grated rind of half a lemoo,and pour over. Beat the whites of ths eggs to a stiff froth, add one tablespoonful of suga.r,and heap over the whole. Set in the Oven until a light brJwn. Serve cold. " Madge."

LADIES LETTER.

My DRAB Nicurz,

Millinery in its present fashion is very pretti and worthy of imitation by amateurs, as +4 presents few difficulties, and the result is good. One fashion which might be imitated is a toque of heliotrope satin straw; the shape with deep brim and Tam O'Shanter crown, much bent and pulled to form a recess for the trimming. Black sequin net is lightly folded round the crown, and forms a spreading bow at the back, a cluster of crushed roses forming the centre of the bow. At the side a black ostrich tip, and more crushed roses under the brim.

Another is a stylish toque of fancy black crino- line, the crown powdered with steel sequins. The brim is deep, and in front is recessed and filled in with soft pink stocks, and above this are sequined bows and two black plumes. The back has two rosettes of black chiffon, powdered with steel sequins, and a jet buckle between. Another style is a " Peter the Great's " hat of fancy blue straw, draped with blue chitlon, and the back filled in with three rosettes of blue and two of white chiffon. In front, four white wings and a high, full curled osprey are divided by two large rosettes of blue chiffon placed, one low, and the other quite on the crown. A pink hat, with black wings and osprey, is charming in this same fashion. For cycling, golfing and yachting Vienna felt hats slightly trimmed are best. The long close-fitting skirts now so fashionable are extremely chic with a short plain coat of self- colour, and this fashion will undoubtedly gain in favour as the autumn progresses, and the thin blouses and bodices become unwearable. Plaid skirts, tiny shepherd checks, materials with hozizontal lines, and also plain fabrics are used for skirts, but, if plain, there is some contrast in colour or fabric, as, for instance, a cloth skirt and velvet coat to mark the distinction.

A serge blouse is a useful item for country and seaside wear, and a very smart one can be made with ruchings of white ribbon and a silk cord in- side. The revers should fasten over the bust with a silk tie, and the vest should be of white washing silk, corded in groups, with the fulness forming a deep space between. The back of the blouse is quite plain, with a mere soupcon of fulness just at the centre over the belt. A square collar falls over the top of the sleeve in epaulette fashion, and is tolerably deep at the back, and a belt is worn with buckle at the back as well as in front.

For seaside and country wear in the warm season, morning costurnca showing a combination of plain coloured linen and fancy plaid cambric, with a white or very pale ground, are smart and uncom- mon. Take as au example a plain skirt of pale green linen, with blou9es of green and white plaid in a large broken check, which worn with a white muslin stock, or one of green ribbon, finished with lace, and a white or green belt, makes a very dainty and becoming toilette.

suitat... gown is of sii site batiste, trimmed with insertions of lace. Vest and cuff-pieces of lace to match are also worn. Voile, cashmere, and the lighter makes of serge are all desirable fabrics for early autumn gowns. They can he worn in the country, by the sea, or on touring trips, and consequently the trimmings should not be of too perishable i a description. Braid of the military or tubular kind s admirable in black or cream, and the white linen braids are very generally used. There are also combinations of braid and gimp which are very effective, or lines of braid, satin riblo)n, or narrow ribbon velvet can be overlaid at intervals with applique motifs of lace braid or guipure to simulate an embroidered border. The fine piece guipure of antique or pale darn hue is charming mounted on white satin for yokes, fronts, collars, or plastrons, and these last clean and retain their freshness much better than white cloth, and at the same time are infinitely more dressy and effective. A new but very nice idea for summer coats and skirts is to have the skirt of cloth or serge, and the coat, a fitting one, of satin to match. It is most i popular n fawns, drabs, and biscuit colours, and is very nice for cycling, not to make a record run, but to reach a friend's house for lunch or tea, being a little smarter than if the whole costume were cloth. 6omtimes the coat is an Eton, and dust shakes off satin directly, especially if it be of fairly good quality. Your loving,. DIM&

black

(rTYrigAt.) CYCLING AND CYCLISTS.

No. VIII. PRACTICAL HINTS FOR CYCLISTS ON TOUR

BY C. W. HARTUNG (Casnuntrroa TO THE CYCLIST," "JOU/MAL Das Spows," 131 c.).

Every season finds an increasing number of cyclists in the throes of preparation for their first extended tour, And it is the object of the writer, whose experience a cycle touring extends over two decades, to afford some useful' information on the subject of touring. If possible, the rider should not select a new machine or one with which he is unfamiliar, neither should he make a hurried start upon a neglected cycle which has been lying aside for months. Nothing is more disheartening than to encounter machine or tyre difficulties at the outset. The ideal touring bicycle should be equipped with a brake, mudguards, and gear-case; and, needless to say, should be overhauled before the start. Any needed lubrication or trifling adjustments should receive attention, and, above all, if the tyres have been long in use, they should be detached and examined for any injuries to the fabric lining liable to cause a burst. These remarks do not apply to single-tube or tubeless tyres, but in any case it will be well to ascertain that the valves are in working order. Touring gears should not exceed 66in.

Next to the machine comes the personal equip- ment of the rider. The Norfolk suits, now so widely worn, are peculiarly suitable for touring, affording as they do ample pocket accommodation. In spring and autumn it is well to wear a waistcoat, but during summer this garment may be safely dis- pensed with altogether, or carried in the luggage bag for use at night or when travelling by steam- boat, &c. Good fitting shoes, properly slotted, if rat-trap pedals be employed, should be worn, and the rider will do well to procure a pair made to his measure some little time before starting, so that his footgear may be thoroughly comfortable. The fancy knit cycling hose, with thick legs mid thin feet, answer admirably for touring, and do not shew dust so distinctly as plain black stockings. A flannel shirt of good quality should be worn next the skin, although in the hottest months many riders find cellular underclothing a boon. The linen collar must be abandoned while riding, if the rider in- tends to do more than mere pottering. A light cloth cap to match the suit will be found more con- venient than a straw hat for all.round purposes. Unless he wishes to court a chill, the rider should on no account wear anything not lined with wool, If lined at all.

As regards luggage, simplicity is the end to be aimed at. Of course, there are riders whose happiness is linked with the daily worry of sending on a Gladstone by rail from place to place, so that they may enjoy the luxury of a complete change of attire each evening; but, except under special circum- stances, it is beat to preserve one's

INDEPENDENCE OF THE RAILWAY,

and be free to alter the destination if adverse cir- cumstances render a change expedient as the day wears on. About seven pounds of luggage can be carried on the machine without inconvenience, either in a frame bag, or a hold-all attached to the handles. The writer prefers the latter method for several reasons, not the least being the freedom from knee contact with the luggage. It is prefer- able to use a made bag, with its complete arrange. ment of straps and fastenings, because less time is occupied in packing, while the appearance is neater. Nevertheless, many old tourists prefer the plan of wrapping their luggage in waterproof cloth, making two parcels if necessary—one to strap on the handles, and the other to the back-stays behind the saddle. This plan, if carried out neatly, answers well, and enables the amount of luggage to be increased or reduced without inconvenience.

Here is a list of the essential luggage for a tour ranging from a week to three weeks: Flannel or cellular shirt, pair of thin hose, neck-wrap, collars, ties, handkerchiefs, toilet requisites, small cake of soap (if for the Continent), and ',naps. It should be borne in mind that, in the event of his getting very wet, the rider can usually borrow garments at an hotel while his own are being dried; that trifles may be purchased en route, or sent ahead by parcel-post till called for; and that his hotel will provide him with slippers, or rapidly wash anything required. Outside the luggage, ready to hand, a waterproof cape should be strapped. The cyclist was never better off for good maps and road-books than he is to-day. It will be found most desirable to carry a cycling map of the district traversed by the tourist, but, except in touring abroad, the road-book need not be carried. It will be sufficient to read up the proposed route before starting and make brief notes of any important points. Why should the tourist make himself the slave of a guide-writer? His own observation will provide him with abundant occupation and enter- tainment.

Tours may be divided into two classes—straight- away rides; and journeys to a selected base, whence trips in all directions, out and home, are made. The latter style of touring affords the best facilities for exploring a district abounding in spots of scenic beauty or historical interest, while the former enables coast, river, or main-route features to be enjoyed. If, for example, it be contemplated to explore the English Lake district, the Ardennes, or the Dales of Derbyshire, the selection of a base will be found to relieve the rider of all anxiety as regards luggage and hotels, while enabling him to see much that he would otherwise miss. On the other hand, there is much to be said in favour of the constant variety and change, the

===== 0002601_18980812_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
FERSDOM AND ADVENTURE,

of a btg circular trip. Roughly speaking, the straight-away tour appeals most to the young cyclist, more e e circumscribednjy osthe g of co coveringfinds distances;favou r wi'whileth thethe mature, aged, luxurious, or lazily inclined rider. It is obviously impossible to suggest any average distance of daily riding when on tour. Where one rider delights to rise early, push steadily on, with a brief luncheon at mid-day, and cover his sixty or seventy miles before halting for the night; another prefers to take his ease with dignified deliberation, pausing to inspect every town or historical object encountered, to indulge in frequent refreshments en route, and a siesta during the sultry mid-day, and to reach the destination fresh and fit ;to enjoy the evening table d'lulte. Then, again, the direction of the wind, condition of the roads, and the physical character of the country make an enormous difference. The writer has ridden hard all day, covering under fifty miles; while on another occasion he has pedalled seventy miles after four in the afternoon. According to circumstances and individual tastes, from thirty to sixty miles will be found to embrace the distance ridden by the average tourist in a day. Touring cyclists comprise all classes of people nowadays, ranging from the lordly to the democratic; and it is not surprising that with its increasing membership the Cyclists' Touring Club should have been forced to cater for its poorer members, who are none the less determined to take the road and taste the delights of touring. In the C.T.C. handbook, which for the first time in the history of the club is being presented free to members this year, will be found, under the designation of "Headquarters," the best hotels in nearly every town in the British Isles. At a cost of about 10s. a day, according to the special tariff of the club, the tourist may rely on being done well at these hotels. For those with whom economy is the primary consideration, there is the capital little farm-house, country lodgings, and coffee-tavern list, also supplied free to members. No one can now be deterred from touring on the ground of the cost, or the lack of accoz.modation within the reach of a modest purse. But the rock bottom of economy is not reached by the C.T.O coffee- tavern list. Mr. T. H. worked well It practice in the wucts or uonnemanir where hot-; - conspicuously scarce. Enough has been said to make it plain mat tr well-advised cyclist should join

rnE CYCLISTS' TOURING CLUB before setting forth. The subscription and entrance fee amount to only six shillings, and the address of the club is 47, Victoria-street, S.W. If the tourist propose to visit France, membership of the Touring Club de France is equally advisable. There is no entrance fee, and the subscription 6f. (55.), includes the membership card, insignia, and monthly magazine. A line to the C.T.C. office will procure the necessary particulars. A question which frequently arises in connection with a tour is whether an individual rider or a party of two or more derive the greatest enjoyment from a holiday awheel. The writer has carried out tours alone, with a single companion, and with parties of club friends. His most delightful ex- periences have been with a single companion. It is well, however, to have some knowledge of the cycling idiosyncrasies of your companion before- hand. Provided a sufficient similarity of tastes exists all will be well, but ill-assorted pairs not infrequently cause each other exquisite irritation, compared to which a solitary tour is irresistibly preferable. The photographic enthusiast, for example, should not select a speedman for his fellow-tourist, or trouble will assuredly arise. Equality of riding powers,which implies some simi- larity in the riders' mounts, is perhaps the chief point to be considered. A cyclist with the faculty of making himself agreeable to the persons he meets on a tour will seldom fail to enjoy a solitary jaunt, should circumstances deprive him of a congenial companion. Well assorted parties are perhaps the jolliest of all accompaniments on a tour, ensuring as they do a constant change of company on the road.

CHAPTER XIIL AT maze. Meantime, after a long and weary journey, Gillonne had arrived at the Chateau d'Orbec. She had reached Javaon in time to start with the waggon of slates bound for Lisieux; but the master of the Croix d'Or was sullen; he refused to allow her to travel in it, though he helped her to exchange her tired horse for a fresh nag, and permitted her to ride beside the slate waggon. By next day the woman became tired of this slow progression, and went on boldly by herself. She felt that she was beyond the reach of her would-be murderers, and she rode steadily forward till, tired out and exhausted, in spite of several long halts, she came in sight of the wooden- tile-fronted houses of Orbec. At the Castle she announced herself as a mes- senger from the Countess of Laval and Kergrist— forbGillonne considered that her mistress had a right to the double title. She was at once conducted to the seneschal, in whom she recognised Enguerrand Lenormand, an ancient squire who had spent a few years at Longuedec with his young master, the Vicomte d'Orbec. Ile smiled genially when he Saw Gillonne. "Welcome, my good dame," he said kindly. "This is a pleasant meeting; but you would have been more than welcome had you brought your lady with you." "Is my lord Vicomte here ? " "My lord, the Vicomte has ridden to Longuedec to find the Countess. He had gone again to Ghent, or near it, for the turbulent burghers are again in revolt, and a day after his departure came the messenger to announce that your good and sainted lady—whom may God assoill—was in extremis. My lord returned but three days ago, and was sorely grieved by the tidings; he would not so much as repose himself after a long night's ride. Hs called for a cup of wine and a crust, and then he bade Roger and Louis arm themselves and follow him to Laval. He said the Countess would have gone there." " Ah, MO2l Dieu ! that is vexatious!" Gillonne cried. "My lady spent but one night in the gloomy old place—it is like a dungeon, Monsieur Enguer- rand, I was thankful we all came safe and sound cm's of it. You have by now heard of the death of my lady's husband, the Count of Laval ? " "My lord sent back yesternight a messenger wish the news. Mark you, Dame Gillonne, I do not say the news was std; to say sooth I rejoiced over it in Secret, for, said Ito my good wife: The noble lady is freed from a greybeard, and can lawfully wed our lord.' Ah, Dame Gillonne "—he shook his head at her—"you have not grown up as 1 thought you would. Why, at twenty-three what a little dare-devil were you! In those days you would have found some hindrance to that marriage, even had you been forced to invent it. Pardieu! 'twas a shame and a sorrow to mate April with chill October, when our young lord worshipped the ground the young Countess walked on. 'Twas a shame, I say! " Enguerrand paused, out of breath ; the garrulous old fellow was carried out of himself by the sight of his old friend. To tell the truth, he had wanted to marry Gillonne in those far-off days, but she had not fancied the bragging Gascon, as she termed him. She nodded at him with a glance of intelligence in her dark eyes. "I mind me, Enguerrand, you were always fond of judging your betters. Now, it seems to me that it is difficult to judge for them, seeing that we are not made of the same stuff. Your wife, Dame Margot, can do much for you; it was, therefore, well to be near of an age at the start, and to grow old together; you can thus make allowance for her declining strength. In my time I have seen a young wife ask too much from an old husband; and in like manner an old man will forget that a young woman needs snore amusement than he cares for. In people of our condition it will be so; we live nearer one another; but the great are not troubled by these petty needs. A Countess has to bear children, it is true, like other women ; but so long as she looks handsome and smiling, and pleases her lord, I know not what more she can do for him in his lifetime ; she is certainly not called on to work for him." Enguerrand looked dissatisfied. "It may be as you say, dame; I mind me that for a woman you used to have a passing shrewd wit; but I say that a young girl, be she lady or peasant born, has a right to some joy in her youth. I know right well that is not the Breton creed; in your country, dame, a girl puts on the collar when she weds, and she is kept at work till Death mercifully takes her out of harness ; but, mind you. I come from the sunny South, where we laugh and talk and never give care a parch. And that minds me that you must need food and drink. Come away, come away to Dame Margot. And afterwards we will see what can be done for you." He led the way to the still-room, where sunshine came streaming in through a deeply - splayed window set rather high in the wall, so that little old Margot, with -her bright face yellow and rosy as a shrivelled pippin, had had to mount a three-legged stool to spread a bundle of lavender stalks to dry in the sunshine that fell on the broad sill. At which sight Enguerrand bustled forward and snatched at Margot's black stuff skirt, while he cried: "Have a care, Margot; , have a care! 'Tis easier to break a limb than to set it; and Malapert Jean has gone with my lord, and in his absence there is not one among us skilled in chirurgery." The little woman jumped deftly off the stool; she had begun to make her husband a mocking reverence, when she caught sight of the tall, dark- browed Breton woman. "Holy saints!" she muttered, and crossed her- self; then, with an appealing glance at her grinning hnsband : "Madame, I am your humble servant. Be welcome, I pray you. Enguerrand "—sharply —"give yourself, then, the trouble to say who the lady is who thus condescends to visit me." Gillonne took the words out of Enguerrand's mouth. "Your pardon, madame. lam not a lady, but the tirewoman of that saiated lady the Countess Kergrist, and now in waiting on madame's daughter, Madame the Countess of Laval." Margot looked at her keenly; then she chuckled. "Oho! you are, then, Gillonne, of whom I have heard so much, dame." She laughed merrily. "There was once a time,—'tis true we had only been wed a year—when he would say; 'Ah, awn Dieu, Margot, you should .see Gillonne do that ; she would set all to rights' ; but lie has now grown used to Margot and her ways, and I think he is content. You are, are you not, my man ? " Enguerrand took her by the shoulders, shook her as a do,g shakes a cat, and setting her down on the floor, he kissed her heartily on both cheeks. "Was ever such a tell-tale Now, bustle, old girl, and give this poor hungry Gillonne some ' of your good cookery; I'll warrant there's a corner of pasty, or a larded capon, or a stuffed goose to be had for the asking; and she shall have a cup of Malvoisie that would not shame the cellar of Laval." He left the two women together. Margot had always felt slightly jealous of her unknown rival,but the sight of Gillonne had completely reassured her. Margot had been a very preity brunette when she married Enguerrand; a glance told her that though the hard-featured woman before her was a few years her • junior, Gillonne never could have been 'good -looking. "Eli, then, friend Gillonne," she said, "I am glad you have at last come to see us, but I never thought to see thee still a girl ; thou couldest not fancy my man, but there must have been others to choose from. Alt, believe me, it is better to have a man of one's own than for ever to comb the locks of St. Catherine." Gillonne smiled; Margot's simplicity amused her. "Enguerrand looks hale and happy too," she said. "It is plain he has a good wife." Margot reddened with satisfaction. "Maybe, maybe, my friend ; he tells me so." Then she hastened to set food before her °guest, and pressed her to partake of it. Enguerrand soon brought the wine, in which the three pledged each other, and drank, from metal mugs, to the health of the Countess of Laval and that of the Vicomte of Orbec. But Gillonne only waited for Enguerrand to drain a single cup before she entered on the business of her journey. "I may not linger." she said. "Like the churl who turns his back when his hunger is satisfied, so must I journey on till I find my dear young -Reify." "Where do you expect to find her ? " asked the , seneschal.

Given the slightest linguistic attainments, the foreign tour teems with allurements. The extent of

A TOURIST'S VOCABULARY need not be more than elementary in countries like France, Germany, Belgium, and Switzerland. Here again the Touring Clqb stands the member in good stead, for the production of his ticket enables him to escape the payment of Customs duty on his cycle (which would be refunded on leaving) at any port of France. Failing the C.T.C. ticket, a club badge invariably serves the same end—as the writer knows from experience. One little word of advice may be noted by the cyclist going abroad for the first time. Carry your money in English gold, changing sufficient for the day's needs at your hotel on settling the morning bill. The English sovereign is readily accepted anywhere on the Continent. In France the franc goes as far as the English shilling. In Germany the mark (20•40=L1) is about equivalent in purchasing power to the shilling. Touring in Nor- mandy and Brittany offers the most delightful intro- duction to Continental cycling. Without experienc- ing the rather frugal, if somewhat cheaper, regime of the Ardennes, the cyclist in north-western France, which is within twelve hours of London, rides over superbly surfaced roads, through pastoral country possessing a charm of its own, and he cannot fail to appreciate the ancient towns and peaceful villages, with their picturesque inhabitants. Hotel expenses in France range from 6s. a day in Nor- mandy and Brittany to 7s. 6d. in other provincial districts. A fortnight's tour, including rail and boat return, need not cost more than £6.

The best 'seasons for cycle touring, both at home and on the Continent, are the early summer and autumn. July and August may advantageously be avoided. May, June and September are excellent months. Ireland, Scotland and Wales should not be visited too late in the year, rainy weather being then more prevalent.

Misiricorde ! were it not that I am left in charge to represent the high and mighty lord of Orbec, I would myself ride with thee—thou wouldest not say nay, Margot ?—belike we should together dis- cover the Countess of Laval. But patience; who knows ?—even now my master belike is talking with her at Longuedec."

CHAPTER XIV. ROLLAND TO THE RESCUE.

Rolland, Vicomte d'Orbec, had ridden off in haste to Laval, and thence to Longuedec, but he had not expected that he should there find the young Countess of Laval. Rolland had been absent from France for several months, out of the way of hear- ing news, so that the tidings of Jeanne 's double bereavement had troubled him, especially when be learnt at Laval that the young widow had gone to seek the King, under the escort of the late Count's successor, Etienne de Retz. He had known De Retz in the army for a thorough libertine, seem- ingly good-natured and easy-going, but in reality a man who gambled deeply, and who was un- scrupulous in his greed for money. The county of Laval was a fairly rich inheritance, but the lands appertaining to it were said to be sterile. It was not likely that a needy man would lose this chance of winning a rich heiress, when he had every facility in his hands. Rolland ground his teeth at the thought of such a marriage; it was to him horrible that his pure, innocent Jeanne was in the hands of a man so attractive, who had over and over again proved his unfitness to be trusted with the care of a good woman's happiness. No, Rolland knew that he should not find Jeanne at Longuedec; Laval would keep her to himself, and had certainly taken her to Moulins. He knew that she was surrounded by the soldiers of Laval; his present purpose in riding to Longuedec was to find out whether Jeanne had with her any guard of her own, and also to ask how many men Leon, the old seneschal, could raise or spare from the Castle garrison. At the head of a body of her own people, Jeanne would be far better able to cope with Laval—unless, indeed, he had already succeeded in winning her. But even then, unless compelled thereto by Royal command, the young Countess could not again wed till the time of her mourning had expired, and Rolland felt in better hope as the thought came. He had tired out two horses before he at last reached Longuedec. At Laval he had seen both the chaplain and the seneschal; they assured him that Jeanne had ridden away with the Count of Laval and her three women; no male escort had followed her from Longuedec, though the chaplain said it was possible that such an escort might have followed the lady to Rennes, or even to Vitt* and might then have returned to the Castle. The seneschal's further tidings had made Rolland very anxious. He said that the elder woman who had accompanied the widowed Countess had craved leave of absence; and although she left the Castle with her mistress, she was bound for Mayenne. Rolland had determined to visit Mayenne on his return from Longuedec, to glean, if possible, some tidings of Gillonne ; he was sure that the duenna was far too prudent to leave her mistress without good cause. _

- His arrival at Longuedec had been anxiously watched from the Castle ramparts, and he found the portcullis withdrawn, and the drawbridge lowered for his passage across it. Leon, the old seneschal to whom Jeanne had confided the charge of the Castle till her return, welcomed the young lord with affectionate reverence. "Your lordship's face is the best sight I have seen since your Excellency went away from Csetfrec! Ah, my lord, who knows ?—things might have happened differently. Your lordship was the keystone-of the arch; in your absence the building has criunbled."

Rolland walked on in silence till he reached the great hall. "I have come as quickly s I could, old friend; I have been to Laval, but that is not much of a detour."

"I'll be bound my lady was not to be found there?" said the seneschal.

"Do you know or guess whereabouts she is?" "We have had tidings, my lord, but not of late date; a band of those marauders—your lordship has noted them ?—caught Pierre Livet, and they were bringing him this way, meaning to drag a ransom out of his people, his father being a wealthy farmer at Plenare. Between Blois and YendOme theloarty fell in with the Count of Laval and a strong body of soldiers; there was a sharp encounter, and Pierre managed to escape. When he reached Vendome, he learnt that the Count of Laval was escorting our dear lady to Moulins. So, my lord, if the King is still at Moulins, our chatelaine will also be there."

Rolland did not answer; he sat lost in thought. It seemed scarcely likely that Laval would take his charge to the camp at Lyons; yet he would hardly leave her alone at Moulins, which was now doubt- less in a state of insurrection, for Rolland had heard the rumour that a price was set on the Constable's head. "I do not think Gillonne is with her mistress ; she seems to have gone to Mayenne. Do you know whether she has friends in Mayenne ?" Leon looked doubtful. "There was a cousin, Excellency, but no one near enough for Gillonne to leave her mistress at such a time."

There was silence ; then the seneschal said with some hesitation:

"Can it be that Gillonne went in search of your lordship? I have heard her say there were but two men in the world in whom she felt trust, and those were—saving your lordship's presence—the present company. Has she, then, journeyed to Chateau d'Orbec to seek help for her lady? Gillonne liked not the new Count of Laval; she once told me that should anything happen to our dear lady before the Count of Laval came to claim his bride, she should like best to see Mademoiselle Jeanne placed with the good nuns of the Abbaye aux Dames ut Caen; it is, I think, not far distant from Orbec." "It is not very far off; but, Leon, I have no authority which will enable me to take the Countess from her present guardian. She is, you must remember, a ward of the King." "Yes, worse luck! " Leon muttered between his teeth. lie had been for too many years accustomed to strict decorum to speak lightly of the doings of his betters. Then he remembered that the opportunity he had wished for was given him, and that he was passing it by. "I crave pardon, my lord, but it seems to an old man like me, whose place it is to watch the game in which others play their parts, that if my lord but held up a finger of warning Mademoisalle Jeanne would follow his counsel. Ah, me the old name fits the tongue better than the new one. It was sad to learn that she, so young and fair a lady, was so early widowed; but if it was to be, I say, so best, before she had learnt to love her old lord, or mayhap to find out that she could not love him, dear child! Your lordship knows that I was here before she came to gladden the lives of us all."

"I have not seen her for years past." Rolland spoke dreamily, and it seemed to him strange that he should have allowed months, even years, to pass without revisiting Longuedec. It was true that his duties in the Low Countries had kept him or some time absent, and that when he returned he had found much to set in order at Orbec ; now, however, he told himself he had

always been reaay to and excuse for staying out of Brittany. Presently he inquired of Lion the possibility of raising a bodyguard of Jeanne's own men for her greater security. "The King will probably exact such a levy," he said, "and it may be with less regard for the security of the garrison. Ido not wish to weaken your resources Lion, but there were several stout lads I remember in the village, who must by now be grown men; see to it, old friend. I think one and all will be proud to serve their lady, though they might not care to fight under the banner of Laval."

"There are not many, ,my lord; the Bing has been gathering soldiers from every part of France= and Brittany is reckoned with Prance nowadays; but Lao and Mathieu are still in the village, and they are ready to take service again; only—only" —he lowered his voice—"they will not serve against the Constable de Bourbon." "I understand. You may tell them there is no fear. lam not going to Lyons till I have found your lady; there will be plenty of time for me to discharge them if they do not wish to join the King's troops." My lord does not think that the noble Duke would ever draw sword against the King, his own cousin ? "

"I hope not, good Leon; but it is not safe to become surety for princes. Now, go you down the hill, and brink me as many recruits as you can. Mark you, they must be soldiers, not raw boys. I can give you what remains of to-day; I must start at daybreak." He was going meantime to Coetfrec, but first he wandered into the garden where he and Jeanne had Spent so many hours together. Nothing was changed, except that the flowers were scarcer and lees cared for; he guessed that her mother's last weeks of illness had hindered Jeanne's loving care for her plants and flowers. He seemed to see the sweet, bright face, with its saucy, mock-defiant expres- sion, as they laughed and Jested together. Should he know her. now, he wondered? With so much early promise she had perhaps blossomed out into a beautiful woman. As the picture of her possible loveliness grew in his mind, a truth dawned on him. He saw, as in a sudden flash, the true meaning of his unwillingness to visit Longuedeo. He wanted Jeanne for himself, and he had not cared to look on her while she belonged to the Count of Laval. He smiled at this discovery; it seemed impossible that his hopes and feelings should have centred themselves on a mere child, yet he knew that he had never seen any other young gill worthy to be named in the same breath with his pupil. He paced up and down the little lonely garden; he told himself here ;Was the secret cause of his indifference to other female ittractions. In Italy be had ieen some very beautiful women, but be had remained proof against their charms.

"It is at best but an idle dream; Jeanne will not do the same thing twice. lam past thirty, and she is not yet eighteen. She will, if she is allowed to choose for herself, take a young husband. What chance have I against a gay cavalier like the new Count of Laval? ' Rolland was too much a man of action to spend time in self-torment. He went to the Castle yard and ordered his hap and one of his squires to be ready; then he rode over to Coetfrec. He arrived, as he had hoped, in time to revoke the order he had previously sent, that those of his tenants who had already served under him should at once proceed to Lyons, and await his arrival there; he now decided that they should accompany him to Moulins. His future movements must be guided by any news he might gather there about Jeanne and her escort. _ _

—Rolland felt cheered that he was able to ride •bact to Longuedec with six stout men-at-arms and their following, and when in the everting he reached the Castle, Leon, the seneschal, met him with the news that Lao and Mathieu had engaged to be at the Castle gates by daybreak, with about ten others. "And, my lord," the old man said earnestly, "you may trust to Lao and Mathieu; they are God- fearing men, and in time of peace they stay at home quietly with their wives and children. They will not play you false." Rolland thought a moment; then he said: "I remember Mathieu. He was at Marignano in the Constable's bodyguard. He told me then that his home was at Longuedec. We will make him leader, Leon; the Countess's troop must have a leader till they place themselves under her com- mand. You must furnish them with the banner of Rergrist." Leon looked unwilling; then, as the 'Vicomte smiled at him, he understood. "My lord means that he will lead them, but will not be in command till my lady gives it to hire. My lord will bring us back our lady in safe keeping. Methinks I would be content to die if I could but see- her - once more• aafe- under the old rool._ tree."

"I will do what I can. Meantime, remember that your lady is a ward of the King, and his Majesty may already have provided her with another husband."

(Zo be continua.)

A BELLE OF if-EW GARDENS.

A visit to Kew Gardens at this sca,oo of the year. when all its tropical wonders are at their b t, will afford the keenest delight to lovers of nature's charms. " I am passionately fond of flowers, aid find a great pleasure in walking through Kew Gar&ns," said Mrs. Nellie Dore,a lady of charming appearance,to a "Weekly Dispatch "reporter, " though not long since I feared I should never see the Gardens again. Until two years ago I never had a day's illness,bet thee I had a severe at- tack of rheumatic fever, which taught me what pain was. For three months I was confined to my b.d, and bad it not been for my two little girls, whom I pictured as motherless I would not have cared to live. " Good nursing pulled me through the illness at last, as the doctor admitted, but I found I was then in a terribly weak condition, unable to move a limb, turn in bed, or raise a cup to my lips. At the sliv,htest exertion I would burst into a vio!ent perspiration. For weeks I lay in this wretched state, until one day I attempted to get up when the nurse was downstairs, but I had mis- judged my strength, for I reeled and fell on the floor with a crash. I then began to give up hope, and thought I should become a confirmed invalid, and yet all this time I was longing to be in 'Kew Gardens once more. Then I happened to read of a person who had been snatched from the grave by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and asked the nurse if they would do ins any good ; sbe merely laughed, but Ita inn struck with the idea I wrote to my Mend Miss Jennie Steele, a popular singer, and she brought me a box of the Pills. which I hid under my pillow. I continued taking Dr. Williams Pink Pills, when the giddiness I, ft me and my appetite returned ; in Is as than three weeks I had walnli all the way from here (Mrs. Dore rcsides at 87, S'anhope , Gardens, Harringay) to Kew, right through the Gardens and back again; and my friends lsnow that I have coc- tinued to do so once or twice a week." Although Mrs. Dore is but twenty eight years of age. - she has had eight years of happy married life, and her two children still hays their mother to care for th in. thanks tor. Alderman FAIRCLOUGH in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Gas Committee stated that the Coun- cil would see by one of the resolutions that the commit- tee had decided to reduce the price of gas by threepence per 1,000 feet from the Ist November next. The reduc- tion was due to the fact that their expenses had been reduced in consequence of making the gas at one works instead of two, and to the improved method of coking the coal. The reduction in the price of gas would cost them £3,000. The gas engineer told them that they could safely reduce the price by 4d. per thousand feet but after deliberation the committee came to the conclu- sion that it would be better to make a reduction of 3d. and thus be on tha right side. (Rear,bear.) Councillor LONGTON seconded the adoption of the minutes. Alderman ROBERTS said he was very pleased to notice the proposal of the Gas Committee which he supposed the Council would accept and agree to with acclamation. At the same time it should be clearly understood that the Council expected a good deal more. They hoped and had a right to expect, that the balance of difference between the cost and the charge of gas would be banded over to the Council—or a handsome proportion of it—in relief of the rates at the end of the year. (Hear, hear.) I So long as the ratepayers were responsible for the capital charges involved in this concern, they were en- titled to some relief as well as relief as gas consumers. He hoped the committee—which seemed disposed to act on a fair principle—would, as far as possible, pretty nearly equalise whatever concessions they had to make in the price of gas on the one hand by a ontribution to the relief a the rates on the other. With reference to the annual report of the gas department which had just been issued he was sorry to see that there bad been a slight departure from the report of the last and proviqua years. In the last report they had a comparative state- ' truant of the cost of manufacturing gas in the various departments from the year 1889 to 1897 but the report just issued only contained the particulars for the years 1897 and 1898. He did not suppose there was any reason for leaving the previous years' figures out, but if they bad been included it would have saved the members of 1 the Council some troulale. He did not suppose there was any other reason for the one: ssion except for want of space. Alderman FAIRCLOUGH: We have only one works now; we lead two in previous years. Councillor LONGTON : You have all the information given in the report. Alderman ROBERTS : I know what I am talking about ;whether Councillor Longton does or not. I am referring Ito that part of the report which gives us the cost of manufacture. Continuing, he said his object in raising the question was to make the report more useful for ref- erence. The MAYOR: Do I understand that the statement for 1897 is missing? Alderman Homan : No; we have that but we haven't the years' figures prior to 1897. Alderman Fairclough promised that the figures which were missing should be included in the report, and that an amended table should be published to be added to this year's report. Councillor SHAW GREEN said the Gas Committee gave the Council a sum of money at the beginning ot the year towards the reduction of the rates, and the committee now came to the general relief of the whole body of gas consumers. If this policy were continued he thought the members of the Council would have reason to con- gratulate themselves on the development of the gas business. In the annual report he noticed that the gas manager gave the receipts as "estimated receipts." (Laughter.) The minutes were then confirmed. WARRINGTON AND THE MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL COMPANY. - - -

Alderman ROBERTS in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the:Rivers Committee which contained a corn- Imunication from the Ship Canal Company declining to allow Warrington traffic to leave the Canal at Run-1 corn Lock,s'ated that the Council would remember that the committee undertook to endeavour to get the Man-1 cheeter Ship Canal Company to make a concession to the traders of Warrineton by allowing them to leave the Canal at Runcorn Lock in consideration of the giving up of toe construction of the penstock at Warrington. In accordance with that promise the deputy chaismin of the committee, the Town Clerk, and him- self had a personal interview with the directors of the Ship Canel Company. The interview lasted i hours in which they used every argument of which they were capable to try and induce the Company to give way on the point before them. They did not meet with very much encouragement because the Chairman of the Ship Canal Company told them that there were points involved other than those which seemed on the surface. In other words, if the company made the concession they would have intro- duced an element of competitien with themselves which would perhaps in the long run be disastrous to them,and therefore they were not juetified in making the conces- sion. That matter was therefore now at an end. From the report of the Borough Surveyor on the sewage dis- charged into the river at Warrington, they would see that the complaints which had been made ' from time to ' tines of the deposit of lime were no longer justified by facts. The Borough Survey or had persistently and con- sistent'y had under his purview this alleged depoeit,and his last report,which had been carefully compiled,proved beyond question that the deposit of lime in the river had ceased,and he (Alderman Roberts) hoped that cendition of things would continue. Alderman SUTTON seconded. Councillor BURTON said he would b 3 glad if the Borough Surveyor's report were presented to the Coun- cil, It contained information which the members ought to be in possession of. During the last few weeks the condition of the river bad been in a very bad state. He did not think it had been worse fer some seven or eight years. lie had been expecting that the Borough Sur- veyor would have given them some other statement. There was certainly a greater nuisance than that of lime in the river, and he would suggest to the Chairman of the Committee that eamples be taken from the river Month by month, both before 'the water enters the Borough and when it leaves it. (Hear, hear.) The Melton said no doubt the suggestion which had been made would be taken notice of. Councillor Thrower said he had not the least doebt that the deputation urged everything they could in favour of the concession to Warrington traders, but if he remembered rightly One reason for asking for the concession was the face that the Corporation was reliev- ing the Ship Canal Company of the expense of the con- struction of a penstock. Councintor BENNETT : We are not only throwing it away but giving the Ship Canal Cornpaay a free tip in the bed of our river. Alderman ROBERTS,M reply to Councillor Tinnion,said the deputation urged the very point alluded to before the directors, pointing out that the Corporation had this item of expenditure on the part of the Ship Canal Company, to offer in exzhange for the concession asked for. RIVAL SOAP

The case of Crosfield v. Sharp Brothers' Soap Per- fumery Company,Limited, rival soap manufacturers was again before Mr. Justice Stirling in the Chan- cery Division on Tuesday. Ills Lordship said the defendants' undertaking should be accepted by the pialutiffs,as sufficient till the trial—defendants in future to distinguish their soap from the plaintiffs by selling it in white tablets and in wrappers to be identified.tbis to be without prejudice to any gues- tion. Plaintiffs also gave an undertaking in regard to damages.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COMMERCE. At a meeting of this Chamber the secretary re- ported with regard to the lay-bye at Stag Bridge tkat be had communicated with the Ship Canal Company, suggesting the desirabily of utilizing it in the interests of local trade. He had received a letter from Mr. A. 11. Whitworth, stating that cer- tain preliminary inquiries would be made, and that a special representative of the company bad been appointed to visit the locality and ascertain the possibilities of the local traffic.—lt was decided to postpone the attempt to re-organise the Chamber until later in the year. when a vigorous attempt will be made to increase the number of members, and to carry out the various proposals which have already been mentioned in our columns.

LATCHFORD TANNERY, LIMITED. The employees of the above company along with their wives and friends went to Blackpool on Sat- urday morning, leaving Bank Quay Station by the 5.45 train. They had a most enjoyable outing the weather being all that could be desired. A splendid dinner and knife and fork tea was provided. After tea Mr. Harding rose to say, he was sure they had all enjoyed themselves, and though that was the first out they had received at the bands of the new company he hoped it would not be the last but that it would become an annual custom, and that each succeeding year would be better than its predecessor. (gear, hear), He thought their very best thanks were due to the secretary of the company for first bringing this trip forward ; and to Mr. W. Tinsley and Mr. C. Tinsley. He had therefore much pleasure in proposing that their very best thanks to Mr. W. Tinsley and Mr. C. Tinsley, Mr. W. W. Eyre and all the Directors, for their kindness on this occasion. He heartily wished the company every success. (Ap- plause). Mr. T. O'Brien said be quite agreed with all Mr. Harding had said and he was very pleased to second the resolution, which was carried with loud applause. The party left Blackpool by the 1340 train, arriving at Bank Quay Station about 8 o'clock, all being well pleased with their day's ex- cursion.

AN ALLEGATION AND AN EXPLANATION. DISCU,SSION AT THE TOWN COUNCIL MEETING.

At the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening a lively discussion took place with reference to the treatment of the Wilderspoal Fire Brigade at the recent fire at Messrs. Rin- mond's premises in Academy-street. Alderman HUTCHINSON, in moving the adoption of the initiates of the Fire Engine Committee said, four fires had taken place during the preceding month. the most serious one having occurred at Messrs. Richmond and Co.'s premises. Since the mecting of the committee a pleasing letter hid been received from the firm bearinz 1 testimony to the efficiency of the brigade. He should have a report to make on a certain question at the neat I meeting.

&!dells-an ROBERTS said he had a very serious matter to bring forward. Ile wished to know whether it was a fact that the police at the late fire at Richmond's tele- phoned to Wilderspool Brewery for the assistance of their engine,and when the brigade arrived at the scene of the fire they were ordered away by the superintendent of I the Warrington Brigade? If that was a fact all that he could say was that it was the most stupid thing that had ever been done by any official. If it was not a fact then it was of the utmost importance that it should be contradicted at once. They had received most valuable assistance from the Wilderspool Brigade, and it was net right on that accountoand also as a mat:er of policy for the fature,that they should be treated in the way it had been asserted. They could not have too much strength when fires brake out,and he could not conceive how un- der any circumstances such a position should have been taken up by anyone of their officials. Alderman HUTCHINSON observed that the discussion was out of order. He had said that he would submit a report at the next meeting. Councillor &saw GREEN : You did not say on what question.

- - Alderman HUTCHINSON said it would be upon the question which Alderman Roberts had raised. Councillor Tiarwrow asked the Town Clerk if he had received a report from the superintendent in reply to some correspondence sent to him (the Town Clerk) from Down. It was hardly fair for Alderman Roberts to have introduced this subject. If they had the rep lrt he referred to a very different light would be thrown on the matter.

Tne TOWN CLEM/ then read the following report from the superintendent, which he said ho had received on the previous day : _

Fire Station,August, 1893. Mr. WIIITTIIIS. Dear Sir :—The following is my ex- planation which you ask me to give of the tr3atinent received by the Wilderspool Fire Brigade at Messrs. Richmond's fire on the 17th July inst. We had five hydrants to work at tho Buttermarket street end of Academy-street and four branches from our large steamer"Captain"at the Mersey-street end supplied by hydrants from Mersey-street from 5.30 a.m. About seven o'clock or later we had the fire under control and without any danger of spreading to the adjoining work- shops or buildings, and h ad taken off two jets from the steamer, when Engineer Ratcliffe reported the Wildes- pool Fire Brigade's arrival. I sent him to tell them they had no need to start, we had the fire under control. He reported they had gone away. Sometime afterwards someone reported they had taken their engine round and had pitched in Mersey-street, opposite dlackhurst-street. Myself and Councillor Dr. Cannel! went round. They had laid a line of hose over the roof at the rear of the range fitting shop, and were filling dam from hydrant. I asked Craddock who sent for them. He replied" 1 don't know somebody has." I then told him there was no necassity to start, we had the fire under. He never said a single word to me about plugs, or made any c )m- -plaints be reports. I may state that our other steamer " Major " could have been used had we requir2d further aid. Soon after reaching the fire I asked our deputy superintendent if he required it fcr the Buttermarket side. Ile reported the hydrants were quite sufficient to keep the fir in check. _

I have, since I received your letter, called the mem- bers of this brigade together, and questioned them about not showing the Widerspool Brigade the where- abouts of plugs. I was surprised to know that only two men and myself had seen them, and these had not been asked by anyone connected with the Wilderspool Brig- ade about plugs. I can assure you, Sir, I only applied the common and general rule with all fire brigades, the brigade on the spot first continues work if able to man- age. I may remind you that the same thing occurred at Messrs. Fairclough's fire, February 10th,1893,and at Mr. Thomas Daintith's fire, Bridge-street, August 7th, 1898. We bad these fires in hand when they arrived, and there I were no complaints then or annoyance caused or re- ported. I also report since I have been superintendent of this brigade we have met the Widerspool Brigade upon nine occasione, have worked wi.h them at four fires three of these we have left them in charge, at two fires they had started work and we were not required,and on one occasion I lent them three lengths of bore, they not having enough. I may state that I had no knowledge that Mr. Ran- some had sent for them, although I noticed his great excitement at the early portion of the conflagration. After I received your letter I called upon him, and I enclose his explanation.—l remain,yours truly, FRANK TURNER, Superintendent, Warrington Fire Brigade. [Copy]. Richmond ez. Co., Ltd., Warrinr,ton, 29th July, IE9B. Dear Sir,—

Mr. Superintendent Turner has been up this morn. ing to see Die respecting the misunderstanding which appears to have arisen ever the Wilderspool Fire Bri- gade. The report of their Captain. Mr. William Craddock, is right so far as telephoning for the brigade through Ser- geant Bell is concerned, and although I have no clear recollection of the time he spoke to me, I should think it would be about six o'clock, just when the fire,was at its height. I Not knowing how far the fire might spread, I thought it would be advisable to have an engine to spare in or- der that we might bring it into action if neceesary, and therefore consented to the ringing up of the Wilderspool Brigade. I was not then aware that the Corporation had a second engine in the station waiting to coma upon the scene when required. It was a considerable time after the message before I saw the Wilderspool Captain (to whom l did not Speak), and I was not aware until after the firs was extin- guished that the Wilderspool Brigade got their boss con- nected,although I believe I am right in stating that they never had any water. When the Wilderspool engine arrived the fire was well under control,and it was not then necessary for them to commence playing upon it. I am extremely sorry that the Wilderspool Brigade should feel in any way slighted, and I can assure them that bad the fire not been so well mastered by the War- rington Brigade we should have been only too glad to , have availed ourselves of their serviees.—Yours faith- fully, RICHMOND IS CO., LIMITED, W. E. RANSOME, Director. J. Lyon Whittle, Esq., Town Clerk, Town Hall, Warrington. Alderman SUTTON said that was the first he ha l heazd of the matter, but at the same time he quite expected that tae report of the Superintendent would be in aceerdance with what had just been read.Al the Supsrin- tendent was responsible for the Fire Brigade and as if anything went wrong they would look to him as being responsible,they ought at the same time to give him full charge and authority to do whatever he thought nem. sary or requisite on an occasion of that sort. It was not the first time they had had the question brought before them as to who was responsible for the management of the fire brigade on the occasion of a fire, and he thought they had come to the conclusion that in future cases it would be far better if there was nointerference either On the part of the members of the conamittee or the Counci or anyone else with the Superint-ndeut. (Hear, heat). Under the circumstances he could qtaite understand how- it was that the Superintendent had acted as he had done. It was very often the Case that if there was interference it made it very difficult for the Superintendent to give his orders in the way he would wish to do. Too many brigades and to many engines would on occasions be in the way. Be admitted all that had been said with regard to the kindness of the Wilderspool Company,and he was quite ready to admit that they had readered very valu- able services and he hoped they would continue to do ise. He was gait.? sure Mr. Down would arse ween bo understood the whole of the circumstances that it was a mistake on the part of someone perhaps to anxious to get all ths assistance they could. It was a mistake to interfere without the orders of the superintendent, and he was sure Mr. Down would admit the superintendent wag not to blame,.

Councillor SHETHMIST said there was not a single word in the minutes upon which they could discuss the question,. ...

Councillor SHAW' GREEN : There is the fire itself. Councillor Sarruunsr replied that there was nothing about the Fir + Brigade. Toe Chairman promised a full report,and yet they allowed Alderman Roberts to make a statement with regard to it, and talk upon the qisestiou for half an hour. When they at that end of the table got up to speak on whatever was not 0 3 the agenda they were ruled out of order.

ilderman ROBERTS : I justify my remarks by the fact that the fire at Messrs. Richmond's is mentioned. I am glad I asked the question, because it has given us an opportunity of hearing Superintendent Turner's reply, and I have C 31210 to the conclusion that there has been some unfortunate misunderstanding. I know the Superintendent, and I could not conceive that he would have been guilty of such a gross breach of unkindness such as is described by the Superintendent of the 1111- derspool Brigade. Ido not cast any reflection on the latter gentleman ; there has evidently been a mutual mir;understanding. _ _

Councillor LEE said the charge did not come from the Wilderspool superintendent. His explanation was that he woe away from home at the time of the fire. The complaint was not that the brigade were not required, but it was considered that Superintendent Turner might have been a little more judicious in regard to the way in which he spoke to the men.

Alderman HUTCHINSON- he had always admired the willingness of the Wilderspool Brigade to come to their assistance. On this occasion a misunderstand- ing must have arisen, but the explanation should prove satisfactory. The minutes were then approved.

THE promised publication Political Memolr3. Prince BISMARCK'S macuthr will stimulate expectation several countries. There i what surely may be regarded as an increasing apprehension that tile GERMAN EMPEROR lEWy foibid their publicstioll' as likely to contain matters injurious to the State' It is said that OE, real memoirs of Lord 13KA.c°1(5—

===== 0002601_18980812_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
ib, ed

nun have never been published for a like reason. We do not hear anything of the kind respecting Mr. GLADSTONE'S Life and Letters. We are curi- ous to see whether the memoirs of Prince Brs- AuacK, who was the last of the great statesmen to die whose names we have mentioned, will be the first to appear. There must be a good deal in the memoirs of the three men to instruct,perhaPs to startle. The common idea when memoirs of this kind are promised is to say that now we shall get at the truth. But Modern memoirs have a distinct way of disappointing us. The revelations we expect do not come, the incidents described in fuller detail are not new, anA a total change of presentment , regarding some great political event is very rare. There are effective reasons for this change. We really know more of what is going on in national and international politics than our forefathers did. There is more intelligent discussion of events Whilst they are occurring. Diplomacy is more Open. The personal influence of any one person, Sovereign or statesman, is less than it used to be. The Press gets hold of more valuable information. Party discussion drawslout the more minute facts. The result is that there is very little else to reveal, except of a puiely person character. In Prince trsmAnx's case there must be a good deal that is Personal to some out respecting his relations with the late EMPEROR and his father, his quarrels with different German statesmen, and his separa- tion from the present EMPEROR. Probably in his papers and diaries may be found many things that will require judicious editing. Mr. • GLADSTONE'S memoirs can hardly have this Personal interest, though they must be interesting in many other ways—possibly painfully so. Lord BEACONSFIELD'S memoirs would perhaps have cleared up some of the doubtful points in the 'period between 1874 and 1880, and it may be that out of regard for living persons, Lord ItowroN has not finished or hss not published what he had pre- pared. In Mr. GLADSTONE'S case the materials must almost be as great as in Prince BISMARCK' case, but he was methodical in keeping them, and they are more likely to bewilder by their extent than by their disorder. When these three memoirs have been published a period of forty sears in foreign and domestic politics, full of iatricacies and fears, of personal jealousies and P°litical mishaps, will be made more intelligible arld possibly more logical. But it is as well not to expect any dramatic sensations, reflecting on the personal honour of the dead or the living. vve live too much in the open now for the memoir- writer to be able to present history to us in novel and sensational ways, slaying our idols, and white- washing the victims of popular disfavour.

The House of Lords has been emphasising its existence and asserting its authority. Last week, the Lords—or rather 40 of them—rejected the conscience clause in the Vaccination Bill by a majority of two over the supporters of the Govern- ment who voted in favour of the Bill. On Mon- day our hereditary and troublesome legislators re-inserted the clause by 55 votes to 45, and so Completed a little comedy which has both amused .and irritated a large section of the public. The interval between the two acts was filled up by the .reourings of" conscientious objectors," and the ilathful denunciations of the Government side of `'e pLower House. All this tumult has ow been allayed, the Bill has become 417 to all intents and purposes, and 'will now stand or fall upon its merits. But how absurd it is that not the whole of the Lords, only 10 per cent of them, should have the destinies of a well-intentioned Bill, and the weal and welfare of the nation, in their hands. It is galling enough to have to submit to the domina- tion of the Peers as a body, but when such an in- significant fragment of the whole allocates to it- self the hereditary prerogative and coolly defies the people's representatives, we can only hope sincerely that another nail has thereby been driven into the coffin of the Upper House. The measure in question aroused such an infinitesimal amount of interest in the Peers as a whole, that their verdict merited nothing else than the up- setting it has got.

A. sharp rebuff has been administered to the Manx Court of General Gaol Delivery by the hand of the Home Secretary, who on Monday pardoned John Dixon,the ex-constable sentenced to five years Penal servitude for the theft of a sheep. The harsh sentence aroused a lot of feeling both in Liverpool, Where Dixon formerly served as a policeman, and 211 the Isle of Man. So out of proportion were the rime and its punishment that nothing short of a revision and relaxation of the sentence were for .4 moment expected by the hundreds of people who set about organising a petition. Moreover there Zfaz an element of doubt as to Dixon's guilt which "e should in the first instance have had the benefit 121. The Home Secretary in releasing the unfortun- :‘e man has not only made reparation for the 7,llguish he must have endured, but at the same rile has taught tie Manx Court of Justice (?) a 'esson which they were badly in need of.

We learned, with something approaching unto a shock,that the Corporation of Dublin, " unam- rcously,and amid applause," declined to lend any assistance to the proposal of tke Gladstone Me- morial Committee to erect a statue of the de- ceased statesrnan,similar to those destined for the capitals of this country and Scotland, in Dublin. This is the sense of gratitude which the Irish people feel for the years of unselfish labour and devotion spent in their behalf by Mr. Gladstone! They seem determined to hold aloof from every- thing English and sever the few remaining links `which have bound the two peoples to- gether. Conduct such as this ill accords With the reputed warm-heartedness of the Irish character—though perhaps we do the sons of Irin some injustice in judging them by the actions of their Dublin brethren. Not until a fitting monument to the memory of Mr. Parnell has been erected, will the citizens of the lush capital countenance a statue to the great man who slaved so unceasingly for Home Rule. We could icarcely imagine anything so narrow, little-minded, and ungracious as the decision of the Dublin Cor- Poration,and we rely upon the bulk of Irishmen to ase that such a stain is removed from the Na- tional escutcheon.

--- The elation of the Unionists at Mr. Doughty's 34ceess in Grimsby has been greatly dashed by the I,l.aws from Launceston, where Mr. Fletcher Mout- was triumphantly returned last week. All along the predictions have been in Mr. Moulton's favour, but this did not debar the Unionists from indulging in hopes, if not of victory, at least of a reduced majority. Instead of this, however, the Unionist poll has decreased, which is a matter for some surprise considering the great efforts to Secure every possible vote which Sir F. Mills' supporters put forth. Mr. Moulton came as a Stranger to the borough, and he is a member—a distinguished one certainly—of a profession from Which electors are reputed to dislike selecting their representatives, and yet he was equal to ob- taining a majority of 1088 over a popular local candidate like his opponent. The chief planks in Air. Moulton's election platform were temperance, and criticism of Lord Salisbury's foreign policy. Ile supported the one and attacked the other With equal determination, and the feebleness of the Unionist defence to his pointed thrusts at the Government told much in his favour. Mr. Moul- ton has gone near to creating a record Liberal poll, and his success is another unmistakable evid- , 46,a0s of the trend of public opinion to disgust with "ae Government and all its machinations. --- , The Lancashire County Council met last 'roars- uaYiand passed the resolution of the Technical In- struction Committee basing the proportion of

grants to local authorities on the latest returns as to population and rateable value. Last week we were a little bit in the fog as to the source of these "latest retur:as," but it now appears that for the purposes of the County Council's scheme, the Icounty auditor compiled a return on the basis of rateable value as fixed by the county rate basis of 1897 and estimated population at the present time. There was practically no opposition to this proposal for remedying to some extent the griev- ances of local authorities, who ought to be corres- pondingly grateful for the " graceful concession."

It will be noted with feelings of pleasure by almost everyone that the Council has at last raised itself to the level of other County autho- rites in the country by adopting a bye-law render- ing compulsory the lighting of vehicles during the hours of darkness. Such a bye-law and boon has been in operation in the neighbouring County of Cheshire long enough to be heartily appreciated, and Lancashire's backwardness has for a similar I period been a source of derision and complaint. The highways and bye-ways of the County Palatine will at last be free from a largo propor- tion of the dangers which assailed the individual whom stress of circumstances compelled to travel by night.

----- Some of the leading peace societies are alarmed at Mr. Goschen's speeih of July `22nd,and the sup- plementary naval programme then announced. i This alarm s combined with disapproval, and the hon sec. of the Increased Armaments Protest Committee wrote to the Prime Minister asking him to receive a memorial from the hands of a de- putation. What Lord Salisbury thought of this mild agitation we can only surmise, but his reply was in the form of one of those courteous rebuffs which no one knows better than the private secre- tary of a Cabinet Minister to formulate. It ac- knowledged receipt of the letter, expressed regret at the Prime Minister's inability to re- dtation, aid cfueiive h promised "care: deputation, a ideration of the memorial. With such, the Increased Armaments Protest Committee whoever sympathise se with them, nor athimay be, had to be satisfied. v share their alarm and disapproval, but we should Wewsn s like to ask how much alarm and disapproval would be expressed if Mr. Goschen failed to submit any naval programme or szheme for the country's de- fence. There are two ways of keeping peace, one is by maintang a position of unassailable strength and the other by laying down one's arms and truckling humbly to every would-be tyrant. Most sensible people would prefer that the former method should be adopted by the rulers of this country.

WARRINGTON SCHOOL TREAT

-- The Holy Trinity Day School (Infants) had their annual summer treat on Friday afternoon last week. This year it took the form of a drive to Lymm. A large number of the scholars, teachers, and a few friends, in all 287 were conveyed in eight open busses by Magin Brothers. Leaving Liverpool-road at 230 the party had a mist beautiful and pleasant drive to Lymm and back,arriving home about 5 p.m. when a very substantial tea was provided for all the scholars, many of whom had not ventured on the drive. Everything was carried through in ad- mirable fashion, and a most enjoyable afternoon ended by the distribution of buns and sweets to the children. Great credit is due to the able manage- ment of the Head Mistress (Miss Bee) and her ener- getic staff of teachers.

BRUNSWICK SUNMY SCROOL.TRE&T.

On Tuesday, in delightfut weather, the ata-ranal treat of the Brunswick Sunday School, to the Overton Hills took place, close upon 300 partaking in the out. Arriving at Frodsham at about quarter to two the children marched in procesaion to the top of the hills. After doing justice to a capital tea, both scholars and teachers enjoyed themselves with the usual round of innocent games including races and nut scrambles. The retura was made at eight, and Runcorn safely reached after a delightfully spent afternoon. The resident minister (the Rev. C. W. L. Chriatien), and Messy. Si:nister and Edward Taylor (the school superintenden-,s), accompanied the trip, and , along with the efficient staff of Sunday School teachers saw to the comforts of the little Ones in such an excellent manner that none were " left in the cold." RUNCORN FIRS BRIGIDE SUPERINTENDENT. The following extract from a recent issue of" Fire 'and Water" shows the high appreedatioa of Mr. Rohert Wright the popular superintendent of the Runcorn Fire " Brigade :—" Mr. Wright's servic sin the Runcorn Fire 'Brigade extend from the fire; organisatoa of the brigade. An extensive fire at the 011 Qaay Mill, at which Mr Wright was one of the most energetic help in, brought the brigade into existence. At the end f February 1853, a naanual engine was purchased, and the first appointment made was that of Captain VVright,who took command on March 3rd of that year, and has con- tinued to hold offioe until the present time. During this time the brigade, with its big heavy manna', has turned out to 122 fires in Runcor a and district. Oa one occa- sion Captain Wright came very near losing his life. This was during a fire at the Spanish Juice Works,Frodiham, in 1884. He and a fireman were standing on a high inner wall directing a jet on the fire, which a gable ead fell with a crash, missing the two man by inches only. Cap- tain Wright has, for the last 15 years, been assisted by his son, Mr. William Wright, appointed thputy-superin- tendent in 1883, after four years' services as a fir-min Cap'ain Wright attained his 60th year in December last, and aopeare as robust and active as a man of much younger age, and capable of many years of useful ser- vice. He receiv.d a training as engineer, hut following in his father's footsteps two years aftn comaleting his apprenticeship, took up the business of grocer and baker retiring from active business twenty-three years ago. His leisure time is divided between fire Brigade and Friendly Society (Oddfellows') work.
