RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRHSPONDENr]. As the football seaf.on is rapidly aoproaching naturally all clubs are making preparations to get into working order, and are endeavouring to secure as many and as good men as possible. And as We look round UF,We see that *me clubs have got fairly started and most ot the men have already donned the " war paint "and indulged in a little practice. This foresight will secure for them a few points at the commencement of the season, even if they have to go down later on.

On the old standing of loss of time amateurism— virtually professionalism—something like 4s. a match was allowed for hotel expenses to the players of the Runcorn fifteen. Now comes the question will the team sign for 4e. a match as "pros." or will it be four shillings a week and something extra ? Then again some will require that since it is a ques- tion of pay at all—they should be fittingly paid— paid according to popularity or skill, or even upon some other kind of " sliding scale." There is one way of dealing with the vexed gueation,and that is, pay them a good" nrew" all round, or run a pur 31y amateur team.

The sports held in connection with the Football Club, on Saturday, but for the steady downpour of ram would have been very interesting to look upo.n. As it was considerable disappointment was felt in more quarters than one, at some of the results. Of the local talent, A. Abel showed best form, and el- perienced very hard lines in one of his races. Young Ray (the cyclist) shows considerable promise, but he should use his head as well as his feet, and try and get a machine adjusted to the track upon an- other occasion. A too high gear only means more pushing up hill. Another young athlete of some promise is CL Bell who in the half mile local man- aged fairly well off 25 yards mark, finishing some- thing like fifth, which was a good performance for a first attempt.

D. Anderson the dark horse in the cycle races, never was pushed a single time or called upofl. to show what he really coald do in the way of riding. Charnock, the Warrington rider, was easily beaten, and from the way be rode it was evident he knew it.

At prrsent it is nothing to see a number of young men,boys, and old men—l arrange them in this or- der purposelv,according to the enthusiasm displayed —armed wiai fishing tackle, bait cans and landing nets,haunting all kinds of places where there is any likelihood of fish. Indeed,the fever is so high that in one quarter it is said that a young man with a new set of tackle tried the " Simple Simon" dodge of fishing in a bucket, in someone's back yard. Another came back the other day with a fishing tale almost as long as the fish he professedly caught although he modestly stated that it was not he who caught the shark that appeared in the Mersey.

PROVISIONAL ORDER FOR ELECTRIC LIGHTING

APPIED FOR.

Oa Thursday morning a special meeting, C.! the Run- corn Urban Council W.AB held a-. the Town Hall, when Mr. Samuel Taylor (preaidel). There were also prer:lint : Messrs. D. Bisbrown, W. Collier, E. Gatley, J. Horsfield, J. Villson and Grine. with Mr. F. A. Lake (clerk to the Council). At the request of the Chairman, the Clerk road the following notice convening the special meeting, explain- ing at the same time that it was requisite tirat a month's clear notice shouli be given prior to the holding rf a meeting for the purpose.—" A special meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council will be held at the Town Hall on the 11th day of August 1898 at 10-30 o'clock in the forenoon for the purpose of considering and if thought fit of passing the following resolution : 'That application be made to the Board of Trade for a Provisional Order under the Electric Lighting Acts 1882 and 1888 to authorise the Urban District C3uncil to sup- ply electricity for public and private purposes as (4efined by the Electric Lighting Acts within the Urban District :of Runcorn." The notice was dated July Bth. Mr. Collier moved that the resolution be passed. Mr: Wilson in seconding said that in doing so it gave him great pleasure. There had been some remarks ma le by the ratepayers upon that very important movement. A great number of whom were looking forward with interest and hoped that the matter would be passed and come into operation. The movement had his support. Mr. Grice supported the resolution, which he con- sidered was a very wise courfe to take. From remarks that had come to his ears,he believed that there was an idea abroad that after obtaining the Provisional Order the Council would make it over to the Gas Company. He had a little interest in the Gas Company, so he took that opportunity of saying that all he did in the matter was from a motive of what was test for the town ; and he would willingly sacrifice his little interest for the general good of the ratepayers. (Hear,hear.) Further, he was sure that the Council nor anyone else con- nected with it ever expressed that idea at all. The Chairman could not tell how such an idea had got atrxrd ; and felt sorry that the people of the town had not more confidence in the Council than to say that of them. Mr. Collier : It is a kind of a "holy war "on the part of Mr. 'Lorry. . . . . _

_ Mr. Gatley contended that the Council had lost the opportunity of getting the Provis- ional Order for nothing, or next to it some time ago. but he was favourable to the scheme, and hig,hly approved of the course the Council had adopted. Be took it that now the cost of the order would devolve upon the ratepayers and possibly the cry "bread versus electricity " might be raised just as much as " bread versus baths " was done the other week. When the offer was made to obtain the order for nothing a dead set ek as made against it, but now that they were driven into a ccrirr they had to get it at the expense of the town. _

The Chairman explained that it was not so, and that the people that made the offer were preyared to renew it even than. The Council, however, considered it advis- able that the matter shoulu be taken up and rest in their own hands. It would leave them none the worse off, and they could get Wenty of outsiders to take it off them, or work it in conjunction with them. PFlkingi on's on Wednesday was caught by the machinery and killed. Mr. Eraut, Government inspector of faetories and workshops, was present, and Mr. J. 0. swiit, solicitor, app-ared the interests of Messrs. pilkingtoa Bros.—John Naylor, a fireman at Messrs. Pileington Broehers' sheet glass works, said the deceased was employed at the works in Grove-street. On Wei- neiday afterneon, durieg the Ir avy rain, a snout on the leading warehouFe got choked,anl the deceased went on the roof to clean it. In going alone the spout he had to revolvinr, caught him and killed shaft weieh step over a e • e him. The shaft was revolving fifty or sixty revolutions a minute and was uncovered. It was about eighteen inches from the spuit.—The Coroner : Don't you Wilk it dangerous to send a man up to clean I this rpout when he had to step over a shaft going fifty revoluticns a ministe ?--Wit- ness : We had done it many times before. Continu- ing,he said the last time the spent was choked deceased was the man who unstopped it. Ball had done it four lor five times, as d witness fifteen or sixteen times.— Police-coostable Winnard and John Tipton gave evidence as to the finding of the body, and described deceased's I shocking Lijuries.—Mr. Eraut stated that when he ex- amined the place his first impression w a that it was not a place that required guarding,becluse he supposed that I when a man went on the roof to clean a spout out the ' engine would be stopped. It appeared from the evi- dence, however, that it was a regular thing to do this with the shaft revolving, and that pointed to the neces- sity of having a proper gan ;way over the shaft it that was the way to get there.—The jury returned a verdict of" Aceidental death," with an expression of opinion that greater precautions might have been taken,and that no man should he sent on the roof or anywhere near the shafting &less it was covered. Sr. JOHN'S CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOL. The annual treat to the children attendinz the above school and the Mission school took place on .4aturday afternoon. The weather was not at all of a desirable character for an event of this kind, and the children had, consequently, to suffer. The scholars met at the church at I-30,and after a short service a procession wai formed headed by the Ear:- estown prize band. the Reformatory School band being also present. A tour of the principal streets was made, but when about half way on the journey rain began to descend, and the children had to be hurried forward to the grand stand at Newton Re Course, where shelter was obtained. It was intended that the outdoor enjoyment of the children should be in the paddock of the grand stand, but the rain prevented anything of the kind. Tea was served to the children in the grand stancl,and they had,after- wards, to amuse themselves as beet they could, the dispersal bPing much earlier than it would other- wise have been, had-the weather been flee.

Some rather curious experiments with liquid air have been made by Dr, D'ArsOnval, and the results were reported to the Paris Society of Biology. He offered a guest some liquid air mixed with champagne, and the guest, without waiting for the wine to thaw, swallowed the glassful,which contained about 15cc. of liquid air. The Correspondent oi the " Lancet" says :—" After a few minutes his (the guest's) stomach was actually disturbed, but a sudden violent expulsion of food And gas relieved this condition. It liquid air be poured I upon hand .it ;assumes the spheroidal state and breaks up I into globules, which scatter in various directions. It has been proposed to employ it in diving operations, for the diver carrying a litre of liquid -air upon hia back Would have 1,000 litres of air to breathe.

MANIFEST DESTINY,

(BY MR. H. WHITHAIL.) Quite a considerable amount of guessing seems to be afoot just now touching the future of AmeLica. The European Press is eagerly scanned and its opinions about the war, and the policy the Govern- ment at Washington may pursue form not a little of the American citizen's reading each day. The attitude taken by Germany is a fruitful subject for newspaper comment and takes a big share in the day's gossip. The Press generally, in its attitude towards England has thought it good policy to take a milder tone, although a few of the dailies give her a tpiteful dig new and again. Of course,England could not possibly have assum- ed her present friendly attitude without having some ulterior view, and what could that view be, but one of selfishness ? These trifling incidents we must allew to slide, knowing, as we do, that the exigences of an Ameri- can paper are of a peculiar and arbitrary character. The Irish form a large percentage of the cities' populations and have to be taken into account in the successful management of a daily paper. The priest neglects no opportunity of recounting the terrible deeds England has wrought in the past, and,if his seership is to be depended on, what she will do in the future. The political boss follows suit,indeed he has to,or be would soon cease to be boss. The Irish element must be pandered to

for political reasons, and out of all the panderers the priest is the most unscrupulous. He distorts all history, and makes no allowances. The American Catholic Priesthood is largely made up of Irishmen, and it seems rather apt during this war to call him a true Nine Ann, as thore can be no doubt that his symost lies are all on the side of Spain. From the above it may be seen that the present friendly feel- ing between the two countries is anything but acceptable to the Irish, they would much rather it had been a war with England, and then, how they would have delighted to prove themselves patriotic

Americans. San Francisoo is now a great military camp. Out on the sand hills the white tents of the soldiers are

pitched, and every day the citizens troop out in teousands to pet and bewilder the poor country lads. From the east, the north, and the south the regulars and the volunteers are poured into the city. The volunteers come as raw material chiefly, to be drilled and fitted to play a part in the wonderful game. of war. As they are pronounced ready for the held they are shipped away amid the deafening din of all the steam whistles the city and shipping can turn on. The departure of a troop ship is the signal for a general turnout of citizens, more especially of the sex supposed to be "gentle." The wearine of soldiers buttons and miniature i flags s seen all around. The soldiers buttons are cut, off, and made to do duty as hat pins for the Semething • that the American people call patriotism prompts theta to do a deal that one might well call arrant fd'olishness. On the car, or on the street but one subject is discussed and that is the war, but the manner of it is enough to dis- gust one. Boast! one long boast. " How our ships are amazing the world;""What splendid bravery our sailors are exhibiting." "The Germans, the French, the Russian., Ah ! even the Engleh will consider twice before they insult the stars and stripes. Dewey i.e equ d to Nelson, and as for Hobson, never was such courage seen. With a'l this childish boasting, which obtains among the multituds, there are a few, a very few, who are able to see things from a different pint of view and are not lost to all reason. Of course we have to acknowledge at once that America was un- prepared for war. That she hal no large army, or navy to speak of. Before hostilities were declared there seemed to be a feeling among the responsible heads of the government that more was to be gained by delay than by haste. Shins were to buy. Ammunition was to be found sornewhere,and a thou- sand details teed to be attended to by the Ameriean Government that would be already cut and dried by most European governments. But the reason- able man asks, what about the Venezuelean affair of a little over a year ago. Suppose Cleveland had gone ahead with his insulting message, what would have been the consequence Humanity shudders to think what tragedies the Atlantic Coast would have seen. I read a little while ago of the ',almost wonderful preparednes of the British Government during that exciting time. How the telegraph placed the Government in direct communication with every responsible official in the Britidi Empire, That every ship, and every regiment would have known, almost within the hour, whet its du,ies were. One asks, after the last month's experience,where would America have been? I, for one, would be pleased if thii boastful spirit could die out, but, I am afraid it is vain to expect it. It is taught in the schools, and brought , from the school trathe home. The flag fleets from the schoel, and " 0 d Glory," is the fetish that the girls eqiecially worship, "My country tis' of thee," is on every child's tongue, and so this spurious patriotism is nourished. I have no par iculer objection to patriotism, but I EITII very much afraid it is apt to cause a sort of colour blindness in tie patriot. While the patriot is shouting himself hoarse the contractor is supply- the rotten food to tles army, and clothing to the soldier that rivals in colour the famous coat of Joseph And new we come to the question of " manifest destiny.' I scarcely know what the full meaning of this may be to the general public. To i me it implies an Almighty controlling pewer,and f I be right in this I must confess my inability to see it. If the Almighty is directing the American ship , of State I must perforce think He is going about It 'in a strange way. Especially most I think se if He intends America to incorporate the Philipine Is`ands, and the United States Govern- ment to govern tho.e islands with the product of , the America polit'cal institutions. America stands ' before the world to 'lay as the worst governed country imaginable. This may not apply so much to the Federal Government, but it certainly does apply to State and City Government, During the whole time of my residence in this city there has not been an honest bit of public work done. Not a contract can be let for either a city hall, hall of justice,building or conducting a school. eupplys ing a hospital with food,or medicine, or, last of the list, erecting a depot at the city front, without the most outrageous robbery being committed by those having the matter in i hand. I set down none of this n malice. I merely ask,—ls it reasonable to expect the Ameri- cans to govern the Philipines, when they cannot govern themselves? For some time they have been trying to govern Alaska but according to all ac emits the government there is as rotten as it well can be. Possibly if they had a few colonies to govern, the work woula stand out more promin- ently to th i world,a nd would invite such criticism as would compel better work. Greater New York has been given mere opportunity for organized corruption and incompetence,and Greater America will, I am afraid, but enlarge the sphere of the pol- itician who always asks the question before ern- , barking or any enterprise.—ls there anything in it?, If it is the destiny of America to become a colonizing nation, then,indeed, a great change must not only be worked within herself, but it will work out a wonderful change in European politics. America deems herself quite secure from any European interference. All the European nations - have quite enough to do to watch one another. She points with derision to the utter failure of the European concert and the manifest inability of the' whole of Europe to heal the "sick man," or to keep him in order. And while she is able to point to the want of concerted action in Europe she thinks the Monroe Doctrine will stand as good after she has taken possession of the Philipines as it did .before. She may find herself mistaken, for, although it may be indifferent to England as to what course she may i take in these matters, the rest of Europe s hardly likely to look on with indifference. One can scarcely watch the game 'without some little feeling. I have no sympathy with war. I can only wonder how anyone 'can read, say Zola's " Downfall" orTo.stas " Seleestapool" without being tilled with disgust at the whole thing. Still the horrid business goes on. I find from my paper this morning that the British Parliament cheered to the echo. Mr. Geschen's de- mand tor £8,000,000 to build cruisers and destroyers. Wherever will it end ? I think the American system of raising war revenue just brings out one of its evils in a splendid manner. All over the rail- road sheds are' directions telling shippers where to to buy war revenue stamps. Every article sent by boat or rail must be paid for on ft stamped receipt. The poor man's beer and becca, must even do a share in the good work. In fact-taxes have come to the front in so forceable a manner that at birth, marriage, and death all feel it. In addition to all this taxing as the volunteers arrive in the city from the states the citizens hold themselves bound to make provisien for them and supply their wants. Each Sunday since the sol- diers began to arrive in the city 1 have provided, food for two of them, What a grand thing it is to be patriotic.

NOTES BY THE WAi AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

In the course of his sermon the other Sunday, the Rev. Mr. Carson at Wargrave Church to some extent admitted that the Anti-Ritualists had some just ground for complaint, and that the actions of the Protestant League. and Church Associations are not without cause. Of course one would not expect a minister to say outright that such and such evils did exist in the Church to which be belonged, but when he vaguely hints at troubles in the Church it is easy to gather that the alleged Ritualistic prac- tice have some foundation in fact, and that minis- ters who hold,views of a less pronounced type than their brethren who hold to the Cross and candles and other symbolic paraphernalia, are becoming seriously alarmed.

There is a parable mei.tioned in the Holy Scrip- tures about the ten virgins and the oil for their lamps. Some people might think this was a peculiar subject to descant upon in this column, but lam not going to deal with anything so delicate. I only wanted to call attention to the picnic whicti a number of the local Wesleyans had last week to Danham'J'ark, and I prefaced this note with the parable in question, because to my mind, it was particularly applicable to this particular pic-nic.

So far so good! I don't want it to be thought for a moment that the picnic only comprised ton virgins. Nothing of the sort, but it happens that when they set out on the homeward journey, they forgot the oil, and the consequence was,when come distance on the way, they had to stop to set things right, owing to one of the axles getting hot under the wagonette. They again set off, but it appears they filled the cup with oil before cooling the heated axle, and thus the oil became all dried lip, and the last state of that wagonette was worse than the first. Now had these trippers remembered the ten virgins and their oil, they would have pursued their journey probably without mishap, end cer- tainly with a great deal more comfort.

The jury adjourned to visit the house. On their re- turn the Coroner addressine, them, said they had had an opportunity of investigating the place. He would go over the evidence and they could use their own discre- tion. Oa Friday night Owen and Johnstone had some quarrel and Johnstone struck Owen. Johnstone he thought would not deny that. The fact remained that after he was struck he walked some hundred and fifty yards home. When he got into the house he told his mother that two men had assaulted him. He went to bed and she heard him go downstairs about half past five the next morning. The mother seemed to be a de- cent straightforward woman, and did not want to etty anything antagonistic against the defendant. Imme- diately afterwards she found him on the floor in an un- conscious state. They had heard Dr. Langdale describe the injuries and the position of the various clots of blood and more especially the two clots and some minor ones in a delicate portion of the brain. They had also heard him express the opinion that the injury which caused them might have been iofficted at 11.40 on the Friday night, but that that would sur- prise him, for he would expect from what be saw that unconsciousness wou'd take Place almost immediately. They had no right to send John- stone for trial unless they were convinced that be would be put on his trial at the AS9IZ9B. If he (the coroner) , was sitting as a juryman, he would suppose that he was I sitting,under the direction of a Judge at the Assizes. Then if it was proved to his satisfaction that Johnstone had struck some blow, and it was ale° proved deceased had a fracture of the skull which causei heraorrage in I the brain, and if he afterwards heard the medical men I say that it was improbable that blows given at 11-40 on the night previous had caused the injury, and that it was most probable the injury was caused later, then he would give Johnstone the benefit of the (Roulet. He did not think that any jury at Assize would convict on the face of the doctor's evidence. If they were of the same opinion they had no right te send Johnitene for trial. The only thing then was to form a verdict. The de- ceased had died from hcemorrage in the brain caused by a fracture of the skull. His suggestion te thorn was If they agreed upon that point, to return an open verdict, Unless they wished to consider the matter amongst themselves. If en the other band they carte, they might have the remainder of the evidence put before them. The foretnan said they had made up their minds, and after hearing the doctor's evidence they did not think it worth while ta hear any more. They quite agreed with the Coroner's view, and they would return an open verdict. That, he thought, was the opinion of all the jurymen, and they did not censider there was sufficient evidence to fend Johnstone for trial. The Coroner informed Johnstone that so far as they were concerned he was discharged, bat he would have to appear before the niagietratee On Thursday, but it was probab'e they would discharge him after the verdict which had been given. The Chief Constable said so far as the police proceed- ings were concerned, Johnstone need have no fear of the result.

FURTHER POLICE PROCEEDINGS. THE CHARGE WITHDRAWN. Walter MeCleod Johnstone, a clerk, was charged on remand at the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday morning, oi suspicion with causing the death of William Owen on the sth August. The Mayor (Alderman Wright) presided on the Bench, and the other magistrates present were :—Alderman Blackhurat, Al- derman Sutton, Councillor Shaw-Green, and Mr. Hep- herd. The prrsoner was again represented by Mr. Jen- kins, solicitor.

Chief Inspector Spinks t•aid, I have been directed by Mr. Talto, the Chief Constable, to inform the Bench that an inquest has ben held on the body of the deceaoed man Owen, and in face of the verdict and the doctor's evidence,l have been instructed not to offer any evidence this morning. With your permission I will

_ WARRINGTON FRIENDLY SOCIETIES. GRAND UNITED ORDER OF ODDFELLOWS. BENXI-CHANT STAR LODGE No. 449.

DEATH OF A PROMINENT OFFICIAL. Oddfellowship in Warrington has sustained a severe loss in the death of Mr. Thomas Taylor, at the age of 57 years, who br upwards of '27 years filled the position of secretary of the Benevolent Star Lodge (449) Warrington district. Up to the time of his death he was als3 secretary for the dis- trict and was closely identified with the Juvenile Burial Society. The single-minded devotion he has displayed in the interest of the Order generally and his lodge and district more particularly, has been most conspicuous. In the exercise of his many duties his kindness and courtesy had won for him the respect and esteem of the members. On Sunday about 100 members of the Grand United Order of Oddfellowe met together to pay the last tribute of respect to their late brother. The members met at the Club Room, White Bull lan, and a procession was formed and proceeded to his residence, where tite Oddfellows funeral oratien was read by Bro. J. Keenan in a very impressive manner. The party afterwards fo:lewed the remains t 3 the Cemetery, the Rev. Canon Willis officiating at the graveside. Many floral tribates were sent. it is intended to recognise the services of Brother Taylor by placing an ornamental tablet over his grave. A subscription list for the purpose is now hanging up in the room of Lodge No. 449. A special summons meeting of the above Society was held on taturday at the White Bull Inn,Horse- market-street, to appoint a secretary to take the plaee of Bro. T. Taylor,resigned, but since deceased. I Bro. Charles Easten was elected, and in thanking the members for the honour they had done him 'hoped they would work hand in hand for the suc- cess of the Lodge. Bro. Alfred Lingard was nomin- ated for district secretary. Bro. Joseph Gibson and Bro. Joseph Dutton were presented with emblems for past services and suitably responded. Bro. A. Alceck and Bro. W. Smith were elected auditors. Several members spoke in high praise of the late Bro. Taylor's character and his willingness at all times to do anything for the goad of the Lodge. INDEPENDENT ORDER OF ODDFELLOWS— LOYAL OLIVE BRANCH LODGE. On Saturday evening the officers and brethren of the above lodge took the opportuaity of making a presentation of a nice'y f • ame I Past Officers Certi- ficate to two worthy officers who have done good service on behalf of the odge. The officers pre- sent were : N G George Brawn, P G Broadhuret, (acting V (I), U M Henry Walker, PPDGM Ralph Norman, PP GM Alban Boyle (secretary), P Peter aiegrath, P G Arthur Cotterill, anti others. The Chairman having called upon PPDGM Nor- man to make the presentations, that gentleman in the course of his remares said the Olive Branch Lodge wished to skew P 0 alagrath and P 0 Cot-1 terill that the work they had done for the interest and welfare ot the lodge was duly appreciated by their fellow officers and brethren. He had much pleasure in presenting each of them with a Past Officers Certificate which had been neatly framed and a suitable inscription written upon it, and he hoped that they would both live long and have health and strength to work still more for the bene- fit of the Loyal Olive Branch Lodge and that the presentation might remind them in future years of the many pleasant hours they had spent with the brethren in the discharge of their duty. PG Peter Magrath in acknowledging the presentation saidl Ithat it was gratifying to him to find that he was held in such esteem and respect by his fellow brethren and he thanked them sincerely and said 'he would always be glad to do his best to further the interest of the lodge.—? G Arthur Cotterill said ihe felt proud to be the recipient of such a presenta- tion. The services he had renuered to the lodge he took a pride in. He wished to thank them for their appreciation and he also thanked the Secretary and Treasurer for the assistance they had given him during his term of office. LOYAL OItDER OF ANCIENT SHEPHERDS AU . . . _

The members of the Warrington Juvenile Burial Society and Widow and Orphan Fund of the above named order assembled at the British Workman Public House, Horsmiarket-street, on Monday. The ' occasion being the 27th annual meeting in connec- tion with the Juvenile Burisl Society and the 33rd of the Widow and Orphan Fund. The members be- longing to both funds put in a very fair attendance and manifested deep interest in the whole even- I ing's proceedings. The meeting was presided over by the President, P.P C.S. Henry Warburton, who was suppotted by the Vice-president P.C.S. William Ball. The Chairman expressed his gratification in learning that they were again to receive a very en- couraging report of the past year's transactions. The General Secretary,P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir, in sub- mitting the annual balance sheet showed that the 1 total worth of the Juvenile Burial Society amounted to £l,BlO 19s. Bd., as compared with £1,643 10s. 9.d. for the previous year, thus making a gain upon I the year tranerctions of £167 Bs. 101 d., with a total membership of 1,227. The report further showed that the Widow and Orphan Fund I had a capital of £lOB 12s. 4d,, as against £2OO Os 8d in the previous year. The Secretary, in ' further reviewing the financial position of the society, said that during the past five years thel average gain bad been no less than .21/11 per annum. The auditors P.P.O S. Peter Edgar and P.M. John Jones complimented the members upon the satis- factory condition of the whole of the accounts and testified that the bank books, corporation bends, and title deeds, were produced, examined, and in perfect order. In the election of officers, P.C.S. William Hall was elected president: D.C.P.S. I Frederick Massey,vice-president; P.P.C.S.Lawrence Weir, general secretary; P.P.C.S. James Parr, treasurer; Brothers William Holmes, Peter Edgar, William Eckersley, trustees ; Brother John Jones was re-elected auditor; Committee: Bros,John Jones, Joseph Whitlow, John W. Sykes,Edward Archibald, Thomas Miller, Samuel Bradshaw, Joseph Adams, Jeffery Benion. George Howard, Edward Cliffe, David Harris, Henry Warburton, James Gresty. The remainder of the agenda paper consisted of several matters of importance bearing upon the general welfare of the societies, such as passing of the newly revised rules of both societies which brought forth .some very lively discussion and amongst the principal speakers were Brothers Thomas Miller, Peter Edgar, John Jones, Joseph Whitlow, Edward Archibald,James Parr,Lawrence Weir,W. Eckereley, and others. A vote of thanks was accorded the re- tiring president, P.P.C.S. Henry Warburton for the admirable manner he had conducted the business during the past year. Brother Warburton, in re- sponse said the kind expression that had been dis- played that evening rewarded him for the labours he had spent upon the societies during his term of office and would leave pleasant recollections behind it. The meeting afterwards terminated.

BAPTISMAL REGENERATION AND PROTESTANT BELIEF.

Sir,—The Catholic Faith is not always furtunete in its exponents, and though I take no exception to the state- ments in the reply by Sursurn Cords to loaa's letter on the subject of Baptismal Regeneration, it seems to me that they lack lucidity, and are couched too much in the technical terminology of an antiquated dry-se-dust to be of much assistance to tbe ordinary reader. Now in myself taking a, perhaps, reckless plunge into the limit- h s 3 sea of theological controversy I would aesire to treat your correspondent lona, as being indeed what he pro- fesses to be a seeker aft sr truth,and it is because I have felt similar difficulties. that I desire to point out to him other aspects ef the question. To many thinking Englishmen, who have been brought up, as I have, ucclar what are called" Evan' gelical" influences,there comes a time wnen a broadened outlook upcn the world, and an acquaintance with other forms of thought brings into clearer light the inade- quacy and narrowness of poplar Protestantism. And while some, short of becoming indiffereet or agnostic, find an uncertain foothold in Unitarian or undogmatic Christianity (which to roe seems to present intellectual difficulties of an equally insuperable kind) there are very many, 1 believe, who only wait to have the tradi- tional views presented to them in a more reasonable spirit to enable them to perceive that -what they always regarded as perversions of Primitive Chris- tianity are, in reality, an integral part of the faith once delivered to the saint/. While,therefore, I see that it will be quiee impossible for me to give any_ thing like an adequate consideration to the many con- troversial points concerning the doctrine of Baptismal Regeneration, I think that I shall be able to point out , what was to me a cause of stumbling before I came to give greater consideration to the matter, namely, the strained and false meaning given to many of the words , used in connection therewith. It is easy enough to make I an antagonist look ridiculous by attaching some absurd significance to terms, which he is known to have used in a controversy, and in no matters of dispute is this a more frequent source of misconception than theological. We all stand in need of more definite teaching in the first principles of our religion,and while many are fight- ing about ceremonial details which, surely are only of importance accordingly as they do not sufficiently em- phasize' or on the other hand obscure under an excess of ritual the central truths of Christianity, it might be well for those,whose function it is, to give prominence I to these truths. To my own mind the outward trappings I of religious worship are of importance in that they minister to two very real sides of human nature, the de- votional and the resthetic, and while I deprecate that very eacessive attentien to minute details, which tends to obscurantism and a forgetfulness of the spiritual nature of the service,and while I am.perhaps, not likely to be much impressed by the somewhat grotesque ritualism of the Salvation Army, though acknowledging the good which that body has done, I fail to see the ob- jection to many of the things about which such a fuss is being made at the present time. But to turn to your correspondent bona. Whatever the subterfuges, to which certain schoole of theology in the Church of England have been driven in order t make the doctrine of Baptismal Regeneration fit ia with their particular theories of the Christian life, he is ceatainly right in his discovery that the formularies of the Churets most emphetically enunciate it, and in so doing they give one of the many proofs that the Anglican Commu- nion to which I te'ong,at the Reformation did not sever itself from the belief held from the earliest times of Christianity, however much that belief had meantime been overlaid with adventitious superstitions. Bet I I submit that the stiperstructive, which your correspond- ent has built upon a very simple foundation will not I stand. Now as modern science has shown that in the physical world a gradual development from lower to I higher forms obtains, so the Catholic doctrine has always been that in the rarieriers life, there is a process of growth, very gradual it may ho, and interrupted too often by sad lapses, or, it is to be feared, in some cases arrested altogether. Assuming what to the Christian,at least, is no superstition, the omnipotence and omnipre- seance of God, the Catholic belief that the Almighty nourishes and sustains the spirituel life of His people by various channele, knowa as means of grace, can not seem far-fetched. Grace, help, sustenance call it what you like the Catholic holds may come in all kinds of ways. nee voice of God reproving and enceuragiag may be heard in the words of a friend or in the sermon of a preacher, and too in the sorrows and vicissitudes of life. But above all we have the especial presence of God in the Sacraments whieh Christ ordained in His church, tee Divine Socirte, which he establish, d with tie organize I government, and whice despite all its apparent failures and mistakes Ile he cent nued to guide in its work as His chief, if not only me ens o: reconciling the world to Elineself.Those,who speak of these things as eats a erdinary or miracalous,scem to be oblivicui of the other aoctrines which ties at the very root of the Faith. To what extent are they more extraordinary than the lucerne, tion or the Atonement ? Surely no unaided intellect , could have arrived at ideae, such as these Sil,,gelt. Has I your correspondent never understood that if these Sacraments, or sacramental acts such as confirmation are means of grace it Is the work of God not man,ani if one were to give the most exaggerated importance to the office of the clergy (a thing I ?Mufti hes,ta`e t ) do), one could net regard them as more than instruments in the hands of God ? Has he ever noticed that in the Holy Communion the priest says a prayer,not performs an act of Consecration? Has he ever realivel that the true priest (in the sacrificial eienee), in the Eucharist IS the Lord Jesus Himself ? The Christian life, then, as understood by Catholics begins when the repentant believer at the time ef being beptized is incorporated into the Church, made a member of i Christ's bodyeie adopted into the family of Geri, becomes regenerate or born again : and this is held to be some. thing more than a mere form. And this deetrine is without doubt also, scriptural, primitive, apostolic. ' Need I quote the numerous passages both in the Gospels and Epistles where Baptism is connected with the remis- sion of sins, and where the baptized are addressed as having been thereby made members of the Church? The first Christian sermon ever preached, that be St. Peter on the day of Pentecost, spoke in no uncertain terms as to the necessity of Baptism. Now I hold that in the last instance we must treat the Sacrament as one of the Christian Mysteries, a thing we can , o more explain than we can the nature of the soul : a real belief in Christ neceeserily entails not only compliance with His commands but also a true humility in mind in consider- ing them,however inexplicable,they may seern.Our Lord said " He that believeth and is baptized seall be saved (i e , brought into a right relation to God), but he that disbelieveth shall be condemned (i.e., obviously wilful disbelief is meant.) Now it should be a matter of common knowledge by this time, that saved and con- demned as used above implies nothing as to man's eternal destiny. Man having free-will may not continue saved ' or •in a stare of salvation but may fall away, and 'condemned' means merely his aidul disbelief shall be judged against him. Intellectual differences and ignorance are not here taken into consideration. St. Peter in his first sermon said " Repent and he baptized everyone of you—for , the promise is to you and to your children."So the two preliminaries to baptism are repentance and faith ; the state resulting regeneration. In what sense are these words used ? What did the writers of the Holy Scriptures mean by them? Repentance ' is not likely to puzzle us, but it is obviously out of place in uncon- scious infants. 'Faith' is a word used by theologians in various senses expressing all degrees from a simple belief in the Divine Person and Mission of Jesus Christ up to what has been called a self-appropriating trust in Christ's work. Now it is quite obvious that the higher I degree is only attained by comparatively few nowa- days,end must certainly be the result of a progressive growth in most cases, and there is no doubt that many people have exaggerated the faith which the early Christians possessed in general. I have no doubt myself that repentance for wrong done and an histcrieal or nominal faith were all that was required of those de- sirous of becoming members of the Church. St. Paul in his Epistle to the Galatians writes to rept* eve them for want of faith, they being in danger of relapsing into Judaiereoand these persons he addresses thus • Ye are all children of God by faith in Christ Jesus; for as many as have been baptised into Christ have put on Christ. This statement would not be received with favour in many Nonconformist circles, were it made to-lay. Now anyone who fulfilling the conditions, was bap tized, took upon himself a new responsibility in that he was made a member of the Church, bas so incurred a greater Obligation to live as God would have him. Who will deny, that he, who has been taught how to live,has more to give account for, than he who has not ? Who will deny that God can and will give to bim who thus takes upon himself a new task a special grace to help him ? And this is all regeneration means. The word is obviously used with regard to the spiritual world from the analogy of the physical. Presupposing instruction, belief ,repentance,the baptized enters into a new relation to God, is adopted into His family, is given a special grace to start him in this supernatural condition—never, however, is it implied anywhere that his free-will is abrogated in any way. Regeneration or 'being born agaia' has nothing to do with being 'soundly converted' lor having ' a new heart,' to quote come ' Evangelical- , isms.' Such phenomena do occur and are often very real events in the lives of Christians. But are not re- pentance and amendment centinually necessary for us ? They are peshaps more striking when occurring under I revivals of religious influence and in PeaPle who have gone far astray, but this is not regeneration. Regenera- tion is the condition of all the baptized, supposing the preliminaries have been fulfilled ; the baptized sins against grace and knowledge and has greater respon- sibility. That the degree the responsibility varies enormously in each case is obvious but a discussion of that point is not my present intentions. Now there is no doubt that the early Christians bb tone trgahmt etdhe iinr ct hh iel dsraemn e tboe lbi ee f .b awp ht iesreedi nl n ttehnedyi n gh a tdh ebme etno instructed—the elements of Christian faith and duty. mere is no distinct mention of infant baptism, but I

The shipowners present in the Commons awaited with some interest the reply of the President of the Board of Trade to Sir Edward Gourley. The regu- lations to be issued regarding boys who may be in- denture i to shipowners under the Merchant Ship- ping (Mercantile Marine Fund) Bill are to be care- fully considered during the recess, and the point of the education and housing of the boys is not to be lost sight of; but the regulation will probably be similar for all classes of vessels, whether ste im or sail. The discussion on the clause brought forward by Mr. Ritchie a little while ago, and the corres— pondence which has since arisen,rend-.r the success of the scheme problematical. The feeling seems to be that the steamship owners do not care about it, and, from the point of view of the sailing ship owners, the subsidy is not adequate. There is the minor point whether, if anything in this direction is to be done at all, it should be moved up with the question of the light dues. Altogether it looks as though the whole matter above referred to, as well as the regulations, would be the better for recon- sideration during the recess.

The mll evp clition which Sir John Murray sent out some fifteen months ago to Chri-tmss Is- land tor the purdose of makieg &complete examin• att.:on of that most inte-es lug speck of laud in the In lien tic ;an has now returned to England, Chistmas Island is being exploited commer-ually by a private company, the local agent beim; a member of the well-known Ross family. The total inhabitants of Christmas Is- land,including tne agent and collies from the Malay Archipelago, is, I betieve, eleven,and their only oc- cupation is working the rich phosphate beds which constitute the commercial wealth of the island. From a scientific point of view the main interest of the island is that it is practically virgin, since its isolated position has protected it from the admix- ture of foreign species which meet the scientific student in almost every other part of the world. Mr. Andrews, who was in charge of tha expedition, spent nearly a year on the island, and he will in due course publish the result of his investigations. There is oaly on 3 point on which a landing can be effectedwand some idea of the density of the vegeta- tion may be gathered from the fact that until Mr. Andrews expbred it no one had succeeded in pene- trating more than a mile from the landing-place. Mr.. Andrews himself found two miles a day the limit of his progress through the dense under- growth. .lle succeeded in traversing the whole is- land,but it was impossible to get any general view even by climbing high trees. The cane of the island is,as I have already reported, volcanic, and it seems to have been raised to its present height— about I,2ooft.—by it succession of upheavals,as it is girdled round from summit to base by a succession of fringing coral reefs. Mr. Andrews made a com- plete collection of the animals, insects, and plants, and also brought back many geological specimens. Theinsects are, I understand, particularly interest- ing. Vary large rats and monster land-crabs are tree climbers—a remarkable illustration of adapta- tion to environment.

Preparations have already begun for the greatest army manoeuvres which have ever been held in England. The tro)ps to be engaged will be divided into twa full army c 3/135 (or " armies " for brief) of tare° division each. The Northern army, under the Duke of Connaught, will concentrate at S ilis- bury Plain, the camps being pitched at flomington and Wilton. The Southern army, under Sir Red- ere Buller, will lie between Dorchester and Ware- ham.. From September Ito September 7 these two arrives will be manoeuvred as hostile forces over the greater part of Dorset and Wiltshire. Before the largeri manoeuvres begin, each army will be prac- tised n divisional manoeuvres, the Northern army at Aldershot, the Southern at Wareham. Mean- time, the advance i parties of the Northern army will be gnting their camps ready, and the great mass of the troops will be coaveyed by twenty or thirty special trains to Salisbury, on the 26th aryl 2701 Ins% The whole proceedings will be a series of most interesting experiments, not only in field work, but in the far more arduous tasks of trans- port and commissariat. How to move and how to feed thousands of men, even in rich and peaceful England, is, after all, the hardest of war problems, and I hope that the practice of it here may help us to avoid the wretched failure so often seen among ill-prepared troops in some hostile or desert country. It is satisfactory that a large number of militia battalions are to be brigaded with the regu- lars,

SUDDEN DEATII Or A WARRINGTON BROXER.

The death has been reported to the police of John Gregg, a broker, 64 years of age, who lived at 35, Winwick-road. The deceased had for a lung time suffered from a sovere cold, and had been attended by Dr. Gannett. On Sunday morning he got up shortly before seven o'clock, and was about :;c:, drink some " Pop " when he was seized with a violent fit of coughing, which broke a blo3d vessel. Dr. Adams, being the nearest medical man, was sent for, but Gregg expired before his arrival.—At the inquest held on Wednesday morning by Mr. S. Brighouse, (coroner) in the police parade room. Dr. Adams said when he arrived at the house of the deceased on Sunday the man was dead. He had since made a post martens exanaina'ion of the body. Dr. Cannell was present on behalf of the relatives. There was no external or internal evidence of injury received from a fall, which deceased was said to have had on the 29th July. The case of death was rupture of a blood vessel in the upper portion of the left lung. The cause of the rupture was disease of the blood vessels, so that the death was from natural causes, and Dr. Cannell agreed with him. It was not considered necessary by the jury to call Dr Cannell, and without retiring, they brought in a verdict of death from" Natural Causes." ST. JOHN AMBULANCE ABSICIATION.—THE POLICE AS AMBULANCE MEN. On the evening of August sth at the Bridewell, 22 constables of the borough police force wer ' examined in first aid by Dr. Darwin, of Old Trafford, , one of the examiners appointed by the authorities of the above associatiou. 'The usual lee-Ares had been delivered some weeks ago by Dr. Reginald Smith, police surgeon, and the men had been instructed in the practical work—bandaging and other drill—by Inspector Taylor, who holds the me lallion of the St. John Ambulance Association, having been one of the first men in the force to pass the required examinations. At the close of the examination Dr. Darwin addressed the men, inform- ing that in the way they answered his ques- tions they had showm that the lecturer had taken great pains in imparting to them the necessary knowledge. Referring to the practical work the examiner characterized it as " excellent," and said that from the manner in which the men had per- formed that—by far the most important part of the examination—it was evident that the inatru.ctor was a thoroughly capable officer and had drilled them very satisfactorily indeed. In conclusion, Dr. Darwin urgea the men to keep the knowledge which they had gained always fresh in their minds by continual applies tion to their instruction bo.olca. DEATH OF MR. R. W. COLLIN.

--- It is with deep regret, we announce the death of Mr. R. Wood Collin, builder, cf 12, Marson-street, Warrington, which took place at Menai Bridge, at 7 o'clock on Tuesday morning. The deceased gentleman had not had the best of health lately. but he had been able to attend to his business up to the day of his departure for Wales, which was a week previous to his death. A few days afterwards he became seriously ill, suffering from hamorrhage of the brain,and a rapid development of the disease resulted in his death. The deceased gentleman was well-known throughout this district, and the news of his sudden demise will be receivel with wide- spread regret./n religion he was a Wesleyan,and at-' tended the Bold-street Chapel.He was at one time a member of the Town Council. The flags at the Town Hall and ConAervative Club were hoisted half-mast on the receipt of the news of his death. The re- mains ef the deceased gentleman were brought to Warrington on Thursday. At the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday, on the motion of the Mayor, a vote of sympathy with the family of the deceased gentlemen was unanimously passed. A report of the spe-ches made by several members will be found in another column.

Mr. Gregory, deputy si-ni-i;r-ylns-pe-c-t-c;i:,--rep;Orted that during the mouth 67 notices had been served oa owners and occupiers to abate nuisances, and with the exception of a few cases which would be laid before the Committee, all were b3ing fairly attended to. The district was still clear of all infectious cases, but they had a case of scarlet fever imported into the dis- trict from Liverpool on Wednesday, and they got it away to the hospital the day following. The house had been thoroughly disinfected and stoved, so that he hoped they would remain clear. On the 31st last month he found two large deal dogs in a pit near Newton Cona• mon In a very advanced state of decomposition, and unfortunately the pit was used for watering cattle. He served a notice on the owner to have the dogs removed at ones, and to discontinue to use the pit for watering the cattle. On the day following he again visited the place to see if the nuisance had been abated, and found the dogs had been removed, but the water looked very dark and dirty. On examining the pit more closely he found a discharge of sewage emptying into the pit from a private drain, making the water totally unfit for use for the cattle. He at once reported the matter to the Clerk to the Council, and he served a second notice on the occupier of the farm to abate the nuisance, by at once preventing the swags discharging into the pittand to make proper arrangements to water his cattle with pure water. that had been done satisfactorily, and he trusted no harm would follow. REFUSING TO QUIT —John Brown was summoned for being drunk and refusing to q slit the premises of Henry Wilkinson, Queen Ann Inn, on the 33th July. and also with destroying jugs and glasses to the value of 2,5 6d.—Defendant pleaded guilty.—Henry Wilkinson said on the Saturday night in question the defendant came in his house about 10 o'clock, and wanted to be served. He was drunk and when asked to go out refused, and began to use bad language. Defendant took hold of the table and turned it upside down. He also broke jues and glasses to the amoant of 29 63.—The bench fined him be and costs for being drunk, acd for committing the damage he was fined the dam sge and the cots. CRUELTY' To A HoRSE AT NEWTON. —Samuel Rigby win charged with working a horse at Newton in an unfit state and Jane Johnson was charged witn causing it to be so worked on the 2and July.—lnspector Greatrea who prose- cuted on behalf of the R.S.P.C.A said shortly after 2 o'clock on the Prliay in question, he saw the defendant Rigby in chirge of a grey gelding attached to a flo it. He Saw the animal was in poor condition and on examination after re- I moving the saddle he found an old wound measuring seven Inches which was causing the animal great pain. It also had a bad toot. There was another wound on its off side shoulder the size of a half-crown piece. There was a wound on the off side fore leg, which was causing the animal pain. Defendant said he had come from Parr, St. Helens with it. Witness went to Parr and saw the owner of tho horse Jane Johnson, and she acknowledgel at the time she knew of the sores on the side of the horse. P.C. Clarke gave corrobora- tive evidence. 'the bench fined Jane Johnson 2s and costs and Samuel Rigby lOs and costs. CRUELTY To A HEIFER AT BURTONwoOD,—WIlliain Wars- ley, Burtoawoo3, wait charged with cruelty to a heifer at Burtonwood on the 22nd July. Defendant pleaded guilty.— Inspector Greatrex. R.5.P.0.A., said about one o'clock on the Friday in question, he saw a small heifer of the defendant's I in afield. The animal was about eighteen months old, and was wearing a very gruel yoke round its neck. The yoke was of wo xl with two heavy weights attached. Every time the animal moved in search of food, it was banged on the legs with the irons. Witness Saw the defendant and told him it was a most cruel yoke he had on the heifer. Defen dent said he had put it on to stop it from getting Into another field. After the yoke was moved he found wounds which caused the animal to walk first with One leg lifted up and then another.—P.C. Clarke corroborated. Defendant said the position of the irons on the yolk had been altered since it was taken off the heifer. —Sergeant Cruikshanic said the yolk was just as it was received at the station from the Inspector and P.C. Clarke. It had been kept in a cellar since. The defendant said he placed it on to stop it from straying out of the field. He dll riot intend it for cruelty. The Chairman said they caluiderel it a very bad ease, and fined him 20s and costs.

ALLEGED ASSAULT AT BARLE3TOWS.--Ilaurice McKearn was summoned for assaulting George Harrison, shopkeeper, Barlestown, on the 30th July. Defendant pleaded not guilty.—aeorge Harrison said lie kept a greengrocer's shop. He had an action pending with the defendant in the County Court. On the day in question the defendant came up to him in front of the shop and sent him spinning against some bundles of cabbage, and kicked him and than foll3wed him into the shop. Two witnesses gave corroborative evidence.— Mrs. Harrison. wife of the complainant, said she saw defend- ant kick her husband across some hampers and witness heard him say ••I will give you something to put main the County Court for." The defendant called a Mrs. Williams who said the defendant did not enter the shop at all. Complainant said something and defendant ran at him and fell on his knees at the shop door which was banged in his face.—Mar- garet Musgrove said she was with the last witness, and after I the complainant fell on his knees before the closed door,Mrs. Harrison threw some water on him. Other witnesses corro- borated.—The bench said they were unanimous ire consider- ing the complainant had brought the assault upon himself by his aggravating words, and would dismiss the case. A BREACH OF THE HIGHWAYS ACT.--John Thornley was charged with firing his gun off within 50 yards of the high- way on the 15 July, at Burtonwood. Mr. White, Solicitor, who appeared for the defendant pleaded not guilty.— Catherine Mulaney Amble-lane Cottages, Burtonwood, said on Friday the 15th, of last month she saw the defendant pass her cottage. He had a gun with him, and was walking on the highway at the time. She went to her mother's next daor to ask where the cat was, and she saw Mr. Thorniey shoot the cat with his gun. By Mr. White: she was about 50 yards away when the gun was fired and was not afraid of any danger to herself at the time, Mr. White contended that according to the reading of the act under which the charge was made there was no case its it was not proved that there was danger to any person at the time. The magistrates said their clerk did not agree with Mr. White as to the point in question and the defendant was fined 2s. 6d. and costs, DAMAGING CLOVER.—John Williams was summoned for committing damage to clover root the property of Richard Burn, Golborne. Defendant pleaded guilty. The evidence was that defendant and another man came across the field and they refused to go off when asked. It was a short cut. The damage was Id. The complainant said he wished to press the case as he was annoyed by men passing through the field, The bench fined him 10s. and costs. THEFT OF JAM.—Catherine McCarty was charged with stealing a jar of jam value 90., from the shop of John Howe provision dealer, Market-street, Barlestown. Defendant pleaded not guilty. George Matthew,l3, Taaner-street,Earl- estown.said he was an assistant to Mr. Howe and was behind the counter on the day In question. The defendant came In with another woman and after purchasing some goods she pioked up the jar of jam whilst he was passing behind a partition—James Edward Jeans, cashier. said he saw defendant with another woman make some pur- chases from the previous witness and then pick up the jar of jam and put it under her shawl. He jumped over the coun- ter and confronted her. When he asked her what she had got, she said she would pay for it. Another witness gave corro- borative evidence. P.O. Combs said he charged her with it and she said she was going to the counter for her shawl. In answer to the chairm in one of the witnesses said they had not had a Case in court before, but thefts has occurred and they wished to make an example. The defendant was fined 205., and Costs or 14 days in default. Defendant said she was not guilty and would go to prison. A strange case of sudden death has been reported to the Brentford police. On Saturday night a man named Wallis went home and ate a hearty supper of beefsteak and pickles. The next morning his wife found him dead. A strange eh, cumstanea about the case is that the deceased was found on his back with his fists rigidly extended in a fighting condi- tion. The doctors say they have never seen a man like that In death before.

The St. Roliox goods station,helonging to the North British Railway Company, at Glasgow. was destroyed by firs ou Monday morning. Hints on Cooking Corn Flour. Notwithstanding the wide use of Corn Flour, particularly in summer, as a cool and refreshing Blanc-mange, many fail to bring out its true and delicate flavour. To thoroughly enjoy a Blanc-mange : FIRST.—Make wholly with good sweet milk without any addition of water, and do not use too much Corn Flour. (21 ozs of BROWN & POLsoN's "PATENT" Corn Flour to two pints milk is the right proportion.) SECOND.—BIend the Corn Flour with a little of the milk, bring the rest to the boil, take off the fire, and add the Corn Flour slowly. THIRD.—Then boil well, in fact, boll down to three-fourths of the original bulk, which will take about ten minutes' boiling. To use warm, turn out when cold, and reheat in the oven. FOURTH,—And above all, use only and always the recognised best— BROWN & POLSON'S "PATENT" CORN FLOUR. It is more delicate in flavour and goes further than others. One pound of Brown & Poison's Corn Flour will make twelve one-pint puddings. Thus the cost of the Corn Flour in a pudding is so small, that everyone can afford to have the best. Ask for BROWN & POLSON'S CORN FLOUR —''PATENT!' is their BEST QUALITY—and be sure that you get it.

A Missio LORL• CHARLES EE"S- -n to China. FORD has accepted a some° what novel mission to China, He is going there, at the request of the Chairman of the Associated Chambers of Commerce to re- port on the "future prospects of British trade and commerce with that country." The selection is novel, and it may be justified by results, but it will not very much impress either c.tpitalists or men of conamerce. The impression it will make en Russia and China is certain to be in many res- pects peculiar, perhaps unfortunate. The gallant Admiral is sure to discover before he has long been in the Celestial Empire that he is the recipient Of attentions from the two Powers more pressing thso polite. We may take it as certain that he will be regarded as invested with, secret Imperial powers, and that. the passage in his letter of announce' meet of which he states that he is going "with the consent and approval of his superior at the Admiralty " will be interpreted in a peculiar waY, He was bound to obtain that assent, and he is not likely to visit China without making more genetel investigations than those which are the ostensible object of his mission. He must necessarily enter upon:inquiries respecting railways, ports, canals, andrivers,and this will necessitate privileges which China may not be willing to give. Ile will visa Hong Kong, where there is an important branch of the Navy League, and he ought to visit Shanghai and some important parts of the Yang" tse Valley before he goes to Pekin, or after he hss been there and obtained the necessary autheriss' , tion. We may suspect him of a desire to make trip to Wei-hai-wei and Port Arthur. Concern' ing the latter, it may be said that Russia is net likely to offer him.facilities for what will be re"' garded as a semi-official inspection. It is impnr' bable that he will be permitted to visit the place' at all. For these reasons it may be doubted' whether he is the best man that could have been' selected for a /17483i= of this. kind. He willbe` suspected of ulterior designs wherever, he g,oo* and this may make his mission of very little &JO as a commercial investigation, compelling hien t° pay more attention to subjects in whith, hs ie Fe er, fessionally interested. Otherwise,he may be true/ togive a plam,unvarnishedtale of what he sees, 03 to bring back with him important informat The subjects he will have to investigate prire tail); concern the investment of British :capita4 a ncl the present moment it is seeking ample IMO mainly in two directions—in railways and .mining We shall be doing nca injustice to. Lord CirrAsos BIERESIFORD in saying that his knowledge on the° subjects is not that of an, expert. There, are inefiA in Pekin at the present moment who know In°' about these matters than he does, but they 4re looking after their own special intexests. eommon-sense views Lord CHARMS may take these subjects will be naturally valuable, but are under no sanguine expectations es to the jlCeii hood of men, waiting until he can report,or takivg his views as final on any single point. His miesint/3 is of interest in many ways. It is quixotic, and may become even dangerous. But he is willing t" go, and most persons will wish. him a good hollio and every possible success.

A WARRINGTON TYR": SUIT. In the Court of appeal on Tuesday, counsel ap- applied in the case of the Warrington Rubber Works Company, Limited, v. the Amalgamated Tyre Com- pany,Litnited (of Coventry), for leave t 3 appeal from an order of Mr. Justice Lavrrance in chambers, referring the whole action to the official referee. The action was brought to recover £620 for goods sold and delivered and work done. There was also a counter-claim by the defendants. Mr. Justice Lawrence said counsel refused leave to appeal from his order. The application was granted.

WARRINGTON ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE. A meeting of this committee was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse on Friday. Mr. Wm Houghton was voted to the Chair, and there were, also present Messrs A Jolley, J Ctarke, T Halton, J Johnson, 3- Billinge, with Mr J C Sutton (clerk), Mr S Sutton (assistant clerk), Mr Tunstull (assistant overseer), and Air Owen (valuer). THE ASSESSMENT OF THE LONDON AND NORTH- WESTERN RAILWAY. The CLERIC reported the result of his interviews with the chief agent of the London and North-Western Rail. way Company at London with reference to the assess- ment of the Company's property in the Union. He would ask the committee to pass a resolution raising the assess- ment of the railway lines in the several townships of the Union from £37,490 to £40,490. On the proposition of Mr. A. JOLLEY, seconded by Mr. Ileasseefe, the resolution was agreed to. THE ASSESSMENT OF PUBLIC PARKS. Councillor Straw Green b ing present to appeal against the assessment of some private property, the CLERK said he might as well read in Councillor Green's preseece a letter he had received from the Town Clerk with reference to the • assessment of public parks. Councillor Snow Green was chairman of the Corpora- tion Finance Committee and the 'Town Council had discussed the subject as to who should regulate the assessments. The letter from the Town Clerk said the question of the assessment of public parks had been brought under the notice of the Association of Municipal Corporations and in all probability the opinion of the Courts would be taken upon it. Under the circumstances it would be well to allow the appeal he (the Town Clerk) had given notice of to stand over. The CLERK informed the committee that the case which the Corporation relied upon did not in his opinion relieve the parks of Warrington from the assessment appealed against. It would be open for Mr. Whittle, whenever any decision might have been come to, to appeal again but he thought after several adjsurnments it was only reasonable that the committee should con- firm the present assessment. Councillor SHAW GREEN : But why not accede to the Town Clerk's req.:est arid allow the assessment to stand over? The CLERK : You know what law courts are. It might be two years before there is a decision on the matter. The Committee decided to confirm the assessments of the Corporation parks. FALL IN THE VALUE OF TITHES. The Rev CANON PENRHYN wrote stating that he regrett- ed that a service at eleven o'clock would prevent him attending the Assessment Committee to support his ap- peal. He should be much obliged if the clerk would state the facts of the case. The apportionment of his tithe rent for Houghten,Middleton and Arbury had fallen from £l2O to £B3 16s. 7d. The cost of collection was £3 7s.—The Clerk said it would be correct to alter the valuation to £B4 gross,and take off £3 103.,f0r the rate able value,as the applicant was entitled to the redusiion of the cost of collection. This course was agreed to. THE VALUER'S REPORT. Mr. OWEN in his monthly report st rted that since the last meeting he had inquired into the following matters of appeal:—The hermetically sealed jar syndicate, works Orford-lane, Warrington, gross £l2O ; rateable £lOO. This works is he'd on lease at £l2O per annum whieh expires 30th May, 1899. The glass working portion was stopped in December last, and about the Ist of March the works were entirely clesed in consequence of the valadity of the patent which was being worked, having been called into question and become the eubject of litigation.—The syndicate are entirely hopeful of ' winning their case, and of re-opening the wake. In the meantime they ask the consideration of the committee whilst no work is being done. It is not supposed the case will he called for trial until after the long vacation. which will mean October, FO that the works will be closed entirety for six months at least in the current year.—Messrs. Lipton, Limited, Bridge-street, Warring- ton' gross £132 103, rateable £ll2 103. These premises are held on lease for five years, from the Ist December, 1897. at a rent of £llo,being a reduction from £132 103. The company is now in occupation of the shop and cid- lain only. The firet and second floor rooms which have a separate entrance from Friars-gate are empty, and are in the hands of an agent to let off for office purposes. The company covenants to do all inside repairs. The assessment should be gross £95, rateable £Bl. The report was adopted, and the proceedings termina- ted.
